Table 1
The Curse of Expertise and Performing Under Pressure
1. Q: What does the chapter mean by the “curse of expertise”?
A: Show answer
The curse of expertise is the difficulty experienced people can have when they try to judge how hard a task will be for someone less experienced. Because experts perform familiar tasks fluently, they may overlook the steps, uncertainty, mistakes, and time that a beginner needs to manage. The result can be unrealistic estimates, incomplete instructions, poor product design, or unfair judgments about another person's performance. The chapter illustrates this through technology specialists who underestimated the time novice users needed to operate a mobile phone. Expertise is valuable for performing a task, but it does not automatically provide an accurate view of the beginner's experience. The practical lesson is to deliberately reconstruct the novice perspective instead of assuming that a task feels as simple to others as it feels to the expert.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between being good at a task and accurately predicting how a beginner will perform it.
Key point:
- Expertise can improve execution while weakening perspective-taking about novice difficulty.
- Estimate and teach from the learner's perspective, not only from the expert's fluency.
Source links:
- Hinds: The Curse of Expertise
2. Q: How did Pamela Hinds investigate whether experts could predict novice performance accurately?
A: Show answer
Hinds compared three groups who estimated how long a novice would take to learn several mobile-phone tasks: salespeople who knew the technology well, customers with some experience, and people who had never used it. The novices then attempted the tasks using the written instructions supplied with the phone. Comparing estimates with actual completion times allowed the study to test predictive accuracy rather than relying on confidence or opinion. The chapter reports that the salespeople, despite their product knowledge and customer contact, were the least accurate group. The design matters because it separates task expertise from the ability to understand a novice's learning process. It also provides a practical model for testing forecasts: obtain estimates before work begins, record actual performance, and compare the two systematically.
Study instruction:
- Identify the predictor groups, the novice task, the estimated measure, and the actual performance measure in Hinds's study.
Key point:
- Predictive accuracy must be tested against observed novice performance.
- Being familiar with users does not guarantee accurate estimates of their learning time.
Source links:
- Hinds: The Curse of Expertise
3. Q: What did the mobile-phone experiment reveal about experts' estimates of learning time?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's account, novice users took about thirty minutes to complete the set of phone tasks, while salespeople estimated that the work would take less than thirteen minutes. The salespeople's estimates were therefore substantially below the observed novice time. Customers with some experience were more accurate because they retained a degree of familiarity with the technology while still remembering the effort involved in learning it. The finding is not that experts are generally poor at all predictions; it is that deep familiarity can distort a specific prediction about another person's initial learning process. A manager who uses an expert's estimate without testing it may set unrealistic deadlines, underestimate training needs, or interpret a normal learning curve as poor performance.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the gap between the reported novice completion time and the expert estimate, then explain how such a gap could affect a project plan.
Key point:
- The chapter's example shows a large gap between expert prediction and novice experience.
- Forecasts should be calibrated against actual task data.
Source links:
- Hinds: The Curse of Expertise
4. Q: Why did asking experts to remember their own early learning difficulties fail to correct their estimates?
A: Show answer
Hinds tested a recall prompt that asked salespeople to think about the confusion and problems they had experienced when first learning the phone. According to the chapter, this reminder did not materially improve their estimates. One explanation is that experts may have difficulty retrieving the detailed, effortful stages of their earlier learning after a skill has become automatic. A general instruction to “remember when you were a beginner” may not restore specific forgotten steps or recreate the novice's current uncertainty. A stronger approach is to observe real beginners, ask them to verbalize difficulties, review task recordings, and test estimates against measured completion times. The lesson is to use external evidence rather than relying solely on introspection as a debiasing method.
Study instruction:
- Compare retrospective self-recall with direct observation of a novice completing the same task and identify which produces more actionable evidence.
Key point:
- A reminder to remember past difficulty may not overcome expertise-related bias.
- Observe beginners and measure their actual problems instead of relying on expert memory alone.
Source links:
- Hinds: The Curse of Expertise
5. Q: What is procedural memory, and why is it important to understanding the curse of expertise?
A: Show answer
Procedural memory supports learned skills and routines that can be performed with limited conscious attention to every individual step. A proficient cyclist does not normally narrate each adjustment of balance, steering, and pedaling while riding. Repeated practice allows coordinated actions to become fluent and partly automatic. This is useful because it frees conscious attention for other demands, but it creates a communication problem: the expert may no longer be able to describe all the small decisions and corrections that a beginner must consciously manage. The chapter applies this idea to mobile-phone operation, sports skills, and other complex tasks. Procedural memory therefore helps explain why an expert's own ease of execution is a poor proxy for a novice's learning burden.
Study instruction:
- Choose a skill you perform automatically and write down every substep a beginner would need to learn, including likely errors and decision points.
Key point:
- Practice can make complex skills automatic and less accessible to conscious description.
- Expert fluency can conceal the steps that novices still need to learn.
Source links:
- Beilock and Carr: On the Fragility of Skilled Performance
- Corkin et al.: H. M.'s Medial Temporal Lobe Lesion
6. Q: How does procedural memory differ from explicit memory?
A: Show answer
Procedural memory supports learned actions and routines, while explicit memory supports consciously accessible information such as facts, events, and material that a person can deliberately recall or reason about. The distinction is not that one system is always unconscious and the other is always conscious in every circumstance; rather, they contribute differently to learning and performance. A golfer may execute a practiced putting routine without being able to describe every movement, while a person solving a new arithmetic problem may need to hold intermediate values and rules in mind. This distinction matters because pressure can affect tasks differently. Interfering with conscious working memory may harm demanding reasoning, whereas excessive self-monitoring may disrupt a highly practiced motor routine. Training and pressure-management strategies should therefore match the kind of processing the task requires.
Study instruction:
- Classify several activities as relying more heavily on procedural skill, explicit knowledge, or a combination of both, and explain your reasoning.
Key point:
- Different tasks depend on different memory and control processes.
- A pressure strategy that helps one task may harm another.
Source links:
- Beilock and Carr: On the Fragility of Skilled Performance
- Corkin et al.: H. M.'s Medial Temporal Lobe Lesion
7. Q: What does the case of Henry Molaison (H. M.) illustrate about different memory systems?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes H. M., whose medial temporal lobe surgery left him with profound difficulty forming new lasting explicit memories, while some forms of skill learning remained possible. The case became important evidence that memory is not a single undifferentiated ability and that different neural systems contribute to different kinds of learning. It supports the distinction between remembering facts or events and acquiring certain procedural skills. The case should not be simplified into a claim that the hippocampus is the only structure involved in explicit memory or that all procedural learning is independent of medial temporal structures. The exact lesion was broader and more complex than a simple removal of one isolated structure. Its value for this chapter is to show why people can perform a learned skill without being able to explain its steps fully.
Study instruction:
- Review the documented lesion findings and distinguish what H. M. could not learn from the procedural abilities that were relatively preserved.
Key point:
- H. M.'s case helped establish that memory has distinguishable systems.
- Neuroscience examples should be described with the limits of the evidence in mind.
Source links:
- Corkin et al.: H. M.'s Medial Temporal Lobe Lesion
8. Q: How can automaticity make it harder for experts to teach or document a skill?
A: Show answer
Once a skill becomes fluent, experts may omit actions that feel obvious, skip intermediate checks, and underestimate the number of decisions required. Instructions written from this perspective can contain hidden assumptions about terminology, prior knowledge, screen navigation, or error recovery. A novice may then fail at a step that the expert does not even recognize as a step. Effective teaching requires making tacit knowledge visible: break the task into observable actions, explain why each step matters, show common errors, and provide feedback at points where a beginner is likely to become uncertain. Testing instructions with actual beginners is essential because an expert review alone may confirm correctness without revealing usability problems.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a familiar procedure as beginner-facing instructions and test whether a first-time user can complete it without verbal help.
Key point:
- Experts often omit steps that have become automatic.
- Beginner testing exposes hidden assumptions in training materials and procedures.
Source links:
- Hinds: The Curse of Expertise
9. Q: How can the curse of expertise lead managers to underestimate project time and difficulty?
A: Show answer
Managers who have completed similar work may remember the finished process more readily than the uncertainty and obstacles they encountered as beginners. They may overlook unfamiliar systems, dependencies, approvals, testing, rework, coordination, and the time needed to ask for help. If they impose an estimate based only on their own current speed, staff may face unrealistic deadlines and may conceal delays to avoid appearing incapable. The chapter's airline software example shows a manager discovering that his team needed more time after hearing about technical issues and workload. A better estimate includes the people who will perform the work, a task breakdown, explicit assumptions, and a review of comparable actual projects. Estimates should be revised when evidence changes rather than defended as fixed promises.
Study instruction:
- Take a project estimate and identify the tasks, dependencies, learning requirements, review time, and uncertainty that an expert might overlook.
Key point:
- A manager's personal speed is not a reliable proxy for a team's delivery time.
- Estimate from the actual delivery team's perspective and update forecasts using evidence.
Source links:
- Hinds: The Curse of Expertise
10. Q: Why might people with intermediate experience predict novice performance more accurately than experts?
A: Show answer
Intermediate users often know enough to recognize the task's main steps but have not yet forgotten how much effort those steps require. They can draw on recent experience with confusion, navigation, terminology, and common errors while also understanding the overall task. Experts may have more technical knowledge but rely on automatic routines and an internalized view of the process. Novices may lack enough knowledge to identify all the task components. The intermediate perspective can therefore provide a useful bridge between expert understanding and beginner experience. This is not a universal rule that intermediates always make the best forecasts; it is a finding from the study context that should be tested in other tasks and populations before being generalized.
Study instruction:
- Ask a novice, an intermediate user, and an expert to estimate the same task, then compare their estimates with measured completion times.
Key point:
- Intermediate experience may preserve awareness of learning friction while providing task familiarity.
- Treat this as a testable forecasting hypothesis, not a universal law.
Source links:
- Hinds: The Curse of Expertise
11. Q: How should a manager build a more reliable estimate for work performed by less experienced employees?
A: Show answer
Begin by defining the deliverable and breaking the work into observable tasks, including setup, learning, coordination, testing, review, approval, and rework. Ask the intended performers what they expect to find difficult and what resources they need. Compare their views with intermediate users, prior project data, and expert technical judgment. Record assumptions about scope, dependencies, staffing, and available tools. Use ranges or scenarios when uncertainty is material rather than presenting a single unsupported number. During delivery, compare actual progress with the estimate and investigate variances without automatically treating them as individual failure. This approach converts forecasting into a learning process and improves future estimates as the organization accumulates evidence about its work.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a work-estimation template with task breakdown, owner, expected duration, uncertainty, dependencies, and actual completion data.
Key point:
- Reliable estimates combine task analysis, performer input, historical evidence, and explicit assumptions.
- Variance analysis should improve future planning rather than simply assign blame.
Source links:
- Hinds: The Curse of Expertise
12. Q: Why can explaining a concept to someone less experienced improve the expert's own understanding?
A: Show answer
Teaching requires the expert to organize knowledge, identify assumptions, select examples, anticipate misunderstandings, and explain relationships between ideas. This can reveal gaps that remain hidden during fluent performance. A learner's questions may also force the expert to consider alternative representations or simpler explanations. The chapter describes pairings in which stronger and weaker mathematics students both benefited, illustrating that collaboration can be useful for more than transferring information from the knowledgeable person to the less knowledgeable person. The benefit depends on meaningful participation: simply giving an answer or allowing one person to dominate does not guarantee deeper learning. Both participants should explain reasoning, ask questions, and test whether the explanation works on a new problem.
Study instruction:
- Teach a difficult concept to a beginner, note the questions that expose gaps in your explanation, and then solve a new problem using the concept.
Key point:
- Explaining knowledge can expose gaps and strengthen conceptual organization.
- Effective peer learning requires active reasoning from both participants.
Source links:
- Smith et al.: Why Peer Discussion Improves Student Performance
13. Q: How can peer discussion improve answers even when no participant initially knows the correct answer?
A: Show answer
Discussion can improve reasoning by bringing different partial understandings, assumptions, and elimination strategies into the same conversation. Participants may identify contradictions, test alternatives, and notice information that an individual overlooked. The chapter cites a classroom study in which students answered a concept question, discussed it with peers, and then answered again; understanding improved even in groups where no one initially selected the correct answer. This does not mean that group discussion always produces a correct answer. Groups can reinforce shared misconceptions, defer to confident speakers, or settle prematurely. Good facilitation asks participants to explain their reasoning, compare evidence, and independently answer a related follow-up question to test whether understanding has improved rather than merely shifted by social influence.
Study instruction:
- Run a two-stage problem-solving exercise: individual answer, structured peer discussion, then a new independent question on the same concept.
Key point:
- Discussion can improve understanding by exposing and testing alternative reasoning.
- Verify learning independently instead of assuming group agreement means correctness.
Source links:
- Smith et al.: Why Peer Discussion Improves Student Performance
14. Q: What is meant by “choking under pressure”?
A: Show answer
Choking under pressure refers to a performance decline in a situation where the individual is motivated to succeed and the consequences or evaluation feel important. It is not simply any mistake made during a difficult task. The chapter uses examples from tennis, golf, figure skating, examinations, and client presentations to show how skilled people can perform below their normal level when stakes rise. Pressure may consume attention needed for reasoning, increase distracting thoughts, or lead performers to monitor well-learned movements too consciously. Some people perform better when motivation increases, while others deteriorate; the outcome depends on the task, the person, and the type of pressure. The term describes a pattern to investigate, not a diagnosis or a judgment about character.
Study instruction:
- Identify a performance situation and distinguish a pressure-related decline from lack of preparation, insufficient skill, or ordinary random error.
Key point:
- Choking is a pressure-related performance drop relative to capability or normal performance.
- Its causes vary with task demands and individual circumstances.
Source links:
- Beilock and Carr: On the Fragility of Skilled Performance
- Beilock and DeCaro: Working Memory and Problem Solving Under Pressure
15. Q: Why can a strong desire to succeed sometimes make performance worse?
A: Show answer
High motivation can increase effort, but it can also make people more concerned about evaluation, consequences, and the possibility of failure. Those concerns may occupy attention that the task requires. A student may monitor every calculation while trying not to make a mistake, or a practiced performer may consciously control movements that normally run automatically. Pressure can therefore interfere with the very processes that usually support success. The chapter contrasts performers who appear energized by the stakes with those whose performance declines. The practical implication is not to reduce all ambition; it is to distinguish useful task focus from worry, self-surveillance, and fear of judgment. Preparation should include practicing how to direct attention when the outcome matters.
Study instruction:
- Compare your thoughts and actions in a low-stakes rehearsal and a high-stakes performance, identifying which thoughts support the task and which distract from it.
Key point:
- Motivation can help, but pressure-related thoughts can consume resources needed for performance.
- Train attention and response strategies alongside technical skill.
Source links:
- Beilock and DeCaro: Working Memory and Problem Solving Under Pressure
- Beilock and Carr: On the Fragility of Skilled Performance
16. Q: Are all pressure situations psychologically the same?
A: Show answer
No. A timed examination, an evaluated presentation, a public sports performance, and a rapidly changing operational emergency can all feel pressurized, but they impose different cognitive and physical demands. A test may require holding rules and intermediate information in working memory. A skilled athletic movement may depend on procedural coordination that can be disrupted by excessive conscious monitoring. A public presentation may involve social evaluation, unexpected questions, and the need to retrieve information while speaking. Pressure can arise from time limits, rewards, observation, consequences, uncertainty, or responsibility for others. Effective preparation identifies the actual stressors in the target situation and rehearses the relevant task demands rather than assuming that generic confidence training will transfer to every setting.
Study instruction:
- Build a pressure profile for an exam, presentation, or performance by listing its cognitive, physical, social, and time-related demands.
Key point:
- Pressure is not one uniform stimulus; its effects depend on the task and context.
- Rehearse the stressors that are genuinely relevant to the target performance.
Source links:
- Beilock et al.: Choking Under Pressure—Multiple Routes to Skill Failure
17. Q: How can pressure affect cognitive tasks differently from highly practiced motor skills?
A: Show answer
Cognitive tasks such as unfamiliar mathematical reasoning depend on working memory, attention, and the ability to maintain intermediate information while applying rules. Worry and distraction can compete for those resources. Highly practiced motor skills may depend more on coordinated procedural routines that normally operate without conscious step-by-step supervision. In those situations, trying to control each movement explicitly can disrupt timing and fluidity. The chapter therefore argues against a single universal remedy. A person solving a novel analytical problem may benefit from pausing and organizing the reasoning, whereas a golfer with a well-learned putting routine may benefit from maintaining a familiar external focus rather than consciously checking every movement. Training should reflect the control demands of the skill.
Study instruction:
- Select one cognitive task and one practiced motor task, then explain how pressure-management strategies should differ between them.
Key point:
- Cognitive pressure can interfere with working memory; motor pressure can disrupt automatic execution through over-monitoring.
- Match the intervention to the way the skill is controlled.
Source links:
- Beilock and DeCaro: Working Memory and Problem Solving Under Pressure
- Beilock and Carr: On the Fragility of Skilled Performance
18. Q: What is working memory, and why does the chapter describe it as “cognitive horsepower”?
A: Show answer
Working memory is the capacity to maintain information temporarily while using it to perform another mental operation. It is more than passive short-term storage: a person may need to remember a set of values while comparing options, follow several instructions while checking a result, or hold a question in mind while preparing an answer. The chapter uses “cognitive horsepower” as an accessible metaphor for this ability. Working-memory capacity is related to complex reasoning and attention control, but it is not identical to intelligence, knowledge, creativity, or overall potential. A person's performance also depends on prior learning, strategy, motivation, task design, and context. Pressure can be particularly disruptive when the task already places heavy demands on working memory.
Study instruction:
- Identify a real task that requires holding information while manipulating it, and list what must remain active in mind during the task.
Key point:
- Working memory supports temporary maintenance and active processing of information.
- It is one contributor to performance, not a complete measure of ability.
Source links:
- Conway et al.: Working Memory Span Tasks—A Methodological Review
- Kane and Engle: Working Memory, Executive Attention, and Fluid Intelligence
19. Q: How does a reading-span task measure working-memory capacity?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's example, participants read sentences aloud, judge whether each sentence makes sense, and remember a letter associated with each sentence. After several sentence-letter pairs, they must recall the letters in the original order. The reading judgment creates a concurrent processing demand while the letters must be maintained. This design tests the ability to preserve information while attention is engaged elsewhere. A valid assessment requires standardized instructions, scoring, and administration; a casual version of the exercise should not be treated as a clinical or definitive intelligence test. The broader lesson is that working memory becomes visible when people must retain information while carrying out another operation, which is common in complex work and learning.
Study instruction:
- Try a simple reading-span exercise and identify the storage demand, the concurrent processing demand, and the reason order matters.
Key point:
- Complex-span tasks measure retention while another cognitive task is performed.
- Informal exercises illustrate the concept but do not replace validated assessment procedures.
Source links:
- Conway et al.: Working Memory Span Tasks—A Methodological Review
20. Q: How did the chapter's modular-arithmetic experiment create pressure in a laboratory setting?
A: Show answer
Participants first learned a new two-step method for deciding whether modular-arithmetic equations were true or false. They then completed a further set of problems under a pressure manipulation involving comparison with other students, a promised financial reward, a team-based consequence, and video recording for evaluation. The purpose was to make the task consequential and socially observable while keeping the mathematical procedure unfamiliar to participants. The chapter emphasizes that laboratory pressure was milder than many real-world high-stakes situations, but it was sufficient to produce measurable performance changes. The experiment illustrates how researchers can manipulate pressure while holding the basic task relatively constant, although laboratory results should not be assumed to reproduce every feature of a real examination or workplace crisis.
Study instruction:
- Separate the task-learning phase from the pressure manipulation and identify which elements increased reward, evaluation, and social consequence.
Key point:
- A controlled experiment can compare performance under lower- and higher-pressure conditions.
- Laboratory pressure is informative but does not fully reproduce real-world stakes.
Source links:
- Beilock and DeCaro: Working Memory and Problem Solving Under Pressure
21. Q: What did the chapter report about students with higher working-memory capacity under pressure?
A: Show answer
Students with higher working-memory capacity performed better during the low-pressure practice phase, but their advantage diminished when the pressure manipulation was introduced. The chapter reports that the highest-capacity group experienced the largest performance decline, while the lower-capacity group showed less decline on the particular task. The finding does not mean that having more working memory is generally a disadvantage. Rather, people who rely on demanding cognitive strategies may be especially vulnerable when pressure consumes the same resources those strategies require. The result also depends on the task and experimental conditions. It should not be used to rank individuals, infer their potential from one stressful test, or conclude that high-capacity people always choke.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a person who performs best during practice may show a larger decline under pressure on a demanding reasoning task.
Key point:
- High working-memory capacity can support strong baseline performance but may be vulnerable under some pressure conditions.
- A single high-stakes result should not be treated as a complete measure of ability.
Source links:
- Beilock and DeCaro: Working Memory and Problem Solving Under Pressure
- Beilock and Carr: When High-Powered People Fail
22. Q: Why did some high-capacity students switch from careful calculations to simpler shortcuts when pressured?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that students with greater working-memory capacity were more likely to use a multi-step calculation that required maintaining intermediate values. Under pressure, some switched to easier heuristics that reduced mental effort but were not consistently reliable. A shortcut can produce a correct answer in some cases and a wrong answer in others, especially when it relies on a superficial feature such as whether numbers are even. The important issue is not that shortcuts are always bad; experts use efficient heuristics appropriately when they are valid. The risk arises when pressure encourages a person to abandon a sound method for an untested rule simply because it feels faster. Training should include identifying when a shortcut is valid and when full reasoning is required.
Study instruction:
- Create examples where a plausible shortcut succeeds and fails, then state the conditions under which the shortcut is logically valid.
Key point:
- Pressure may push people toward low-effort strategies that are not reliable for the problem.
- Use shortcuts only when their assumptions have been tested.
Source links:
- Beilock and DeCaro: Working Memory and Problem Solving Under Pressure
23. Q: How can pressure interfere with working memory during a demanding task?
A: Show answer
Worry about consequences, monitoring one's own performance, and trying to suppress distracting thoughts can occupy attention that would otherwise be used to hold and manipulate task information. A student may spend mental effort thinking about the score, the observer, or the possibility of failure while also trying to remember intermediate calculation steps. The remaining capacity may be insufficient for the strategy that normally produces the best result. This helps explain why an individual can know how to solve a problem in practice yet struggle during a consequential test. The practical response is to reduce avoidable distractions, rehearse under realistic conditions, externalize intermediate information where permitted, and use a stable problem-solving routine that does not require unnecessary mental juggling.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a high-pressure task and identify which information belongs to the task itself and which thoughts are consuming attention without helping solve it.
Key point:
- Worry and irrelevant monitoring can compete with working-memory demands.
- Reduce avoidable cognitive load and rehearse a reliable task strategy.
Source links:
- Beilock and DeCaro: Working Memory and Problem Solving Under Pressure
- Beilock et al.: Choking Under Pressure—Multiple Routes to Skill Failure
24. Q: What is the difference between distraction and explicit-monitoring explanations of choking?
A: Show answer
Distraction accounts propose that pressure diverts attention toward worries, evaluation, or other task-irrelevant thoughts, leaving fewer resources for the task. Explicit-monitoring accounts propose that pressure makes a person focus too closely on the detailed execution of a skill that normally operates automatically. The first mechanism can be especially relevant to tasks requiring working memory and controlled attention; the second can affect well-practiced sensorimotor skills when conscious supervision disrupts fluent execution. These mechanisms are not necessarily mutually exclusive, and a situation may trigger both. Diagnosing the likely mechanism helps determine the response: cognitive tasks may benefit from structured attention and reduced distraction, while procedural skills may benefit from maintaining a practiced routine and avoiding unnecessary internal monitoring.
Study instruction:
- For two different pressure failures, identify whether distraction, explicit monitoring, or a combination is the more plausible explanation and justify the choice.
Key point:
- Choking can arise through more than one cognitive route.
- The remedy should address the mechanism affecting the specific skill.
Source links:
- Beilock et al.: Choking Under Pressure—Multiple Routes to Skill Failure
- Beilock and Carr: On the Fragility of Skilled Performance
25. Q: Why is there no single pressure-management technique that works for every skill?
A: Show answer
Different skills rely on different combinations of working memory, procedural memory, attention, physical coordination, and decision-making. A complex calculation may require the performer to slow down, hold intermediate values, and apply a deliberate sequence. A well-practiced golf putt may be harmed if the performer consciously checks every movement. A client presentation may require both retrieval of prepared knowledge and flexible responses to unexpected questions. A technique such as pausing can help one task but interrupt another if applied at the wrong moment. Effective preparation begins with a task analysis: identify what must be controlled consciously, what should remain automatic, what distractions are likely, and what kind of feedback supports performance. Then test the intervention under realistic conditions rather than assuming transfer.
Study instruction:
- Compare a reasoning task, a practiced motor task, and a live presentation, and design a distinct pressure strategy for each.
Key point:
- Pressure-management strategies must fit the task's control and memory demands.
- Test interventions in the context where the skill will actually be used.
Source links:
- Beilock et al.: Choking Under Pressure—Multiple Routes to Skill Failure
26. Q: When can pausing before responding improve performance under pressure?
A: Show answer
Pausing can help when a task requires deliberate reasoning, working-memory management, or selection of the correct problem-solving framework. A short pause gives the person time to identify the actual question, separate relevant from irrelevant information, choose an appropriate method, and avoid rushing into an attractive but incorrect shortcut. It is especially useful when the problem is unfamiliar or when the consequences make impulsive answers costly. A pause should be purposeful rather than an attempt to avoid the task indefinitely. For a highly automated movement, excessive conscious analysis may be counterproductive. The performer should therefore distinguish between tasks that require deliberate cognitive control and tasks whose execution depends on a stable procedural routine.
Study instruction:
- Practice a brief “stop, define, choose, act” sequence on unfamiliar reasoning problems and compare accuracy with an immediate-response approach.
Key point:
- A deliberate pause can protect reasoning from distraction and premature action.
- Do not apply conscious step-by-step control indiscriminately to automated skills.
Source links:
- Chi, Feltovich, and Glaser: Categorization of Physics Problems
- Beilock and DeCaro: Working Memory and Problem Solving Under Pressure
27. Q: What did the physics-expert study reveal about how experts approach unfamiliar problems?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes research by Chi, Feltovich, and Glaser in which physics experts and novices represented problems differently. Experts tended to organize problems around underlying physical principles, while novices were more likely to focus on surface features such as the presence of a spring or pulley. Experts also took time to identify the governing principle before calculating. This illustrates that expertise is not simply faster execution; it includes more meaningful representations of the problem. A novice may react to familiar-looking details without identifying the structure that determines the correct solution. The study supports deliberate problem framing before action, particularly when a problem contains distracting surface information. It does not imply that every task should be delayed or that experts always reason correctly.
Study instruction:
- Take several physics or business problems and classify them by underlying principle rather than by superficial wording or appearance.
Key point:
- Experts often recognize deep structure that novices miss in surface details.
- Identify the governing principle before committing to a solution path.
Source links:
- Chi, Feltovich, and Glaser: Categorization of Physics Problems
28. Q: What is an incubation period, and how can stepping away from a problem help?
A: Show answer
An incubation period is a break from direct work on a problem after an initial period of engagement. The chapter suggests that stepping away can reduce fixation on irrelevant details and allow a person to return with a different perspective. A useful break is not the same as abandoning responsibility: the problem should be clearly framed, relevant information preserved, and a time set to resume. Incubation may be more helpful for complex reasoning or creative problem solving than for tasks requiring continuous physical control or immediate action. The Archimedes crown story illustrates the idea of a solution emerging after attention shifts, but it is a traditional anecdote rather than controlled evidence that every break produces insight. Use breaks as one tool within a disciplined problem-solving process.
Study instruction:
- Record a difficult problem, take a planned short break, and compare the ideas that become available when you return with your initial approach.
Key point:
- A break can loosen fixation and support a fresh representation of a problem.
- Incubation complements, but does not replace, evidence gathering and deliberate analysis.
Source links:
- Chi, Feltovich, and Glaser: Categorization of Physics Problems
29. Q: How should the chapter's discussion of mental energy and glucose be interpreted?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses glucose depletion as one explanation for why sustained demanding thought may be followed by weaker performance. That account should be treated cautiously rather than as a settled universal mechanism. Later reviews of glucose and cognition report mixed evidence and limitations in the available studies; evidence about short-term glucose intake or cognitive performance does not establish that ordinary mental effort simply empties a fixed glucose reservoir. The practical recommendation to allow recovery after sustained concentration can still be sensible, but it should not be justified by an oversimplified “brain fuel tank” model. Fatigue, sleep, stress, task duration, motivation, and cognitive load can all affect performance. Distinguish the chapter's historical explanation from what later research can support.
Study instruction:
- Compare the chapter's resource-depletion explanation with later evidence reviews and identify which claims remain uncertain.
Key point:
- Do not present simple glucose depletion as an established explanation for all mental fatigue.
- Recovery breaks may help, but their benefits have multiple possible mechanisms.
Source links:
- Systematic Review: Glucose, Sucrose, and Cognition
- Neukirchen et al.: Cognitive Glucose Sensitivity
30. Q: What is pressure training, and why can it improve performance in important situations?
A: Show answer
Pressure training exposes a person to manageable versions of the conditions likely to occur during an important performance. Instead of practicing only in a comfortable environment, the learner rehearses with selected stressors such as an audience, time limits, evaluation, unfamiliar questions, or a realistic opponent. The aim is to develop task competence while becoming more familiar with the attention and emotional demands that accompany pressure. The chapter describes police officers who practiced against an opponent using marking cartridges and later showed less performance deterioration than officers who practiced only against stationary targets. Pressure training should be progressive, safe, ethical, and specific to the task. It should not introduce unnecessary danger or assume that one exposure automatically creates resilience.
Study instruction:
- Design a graduated practice plan that adds realistic pressure only after the basic skill can be performed safely and consistently.
Key point:
- Rehearsing relevant stressors can improve readiness for high-pressure performance.
- Pressure exposure must be controlled, progressive, and appropriate to the skill.
Source links:
- Oudejans: Reality-Based Practice Under Pressure
31. Q: What did Oudejans's police-shooting study show about practice under pressure?
A: Show answer
The study compared police officers who practiced shooting at an opponent returning fire with marking cartridges against officers who practiced only on stationary cardboard targets. Both groups initially performed worse under the opponent condition. After the training sessions, the group that practiced under pressure no longer showed the same deterioration when facing the opponent, whereas the control group's performance remained more affected. The study suggests that task-relevant pressure practice can help performers adapt to some stressors. Its sample was small and the result concerns a specific police-shooting task and training arrangement. It should not be generalized without qualification to every sport, workplace, or emergency response. The chapter uses it to illustrate the principle of practicing in conditions that resemble the demands of the real performance.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the experimental and control conditions, the training exposure, and the difference observed at post-test.
Key point:
- The pressure-practice group adapted better to the tested opponent condition.
- Transfer depends on the task, training design, and similarity between practice and performance.
Source links:
- Oudejans: Reality-Based Practice Under Pressure
32. Q: Why can mild stress during practice prepare someone for more intense pressure later?
A: Show answer
Mild, controlled stress can make some features of the target situation familiar before the performer encounters the full stakes. An audience during rehearsal, a timed practice round, a realistic question-and-answer session, or a simulated opponent can help the learner practice maintaining attention while being observed or evaluated. The objective is not to maximize anxiety; excessive stress can impair learning or create unsafe behavior. A useful progression begins with stable basic performance, adds one manageable stressor, reviews the result, and increases complexity only when the learner can recover and perform safely. The chapter's examples support task-specific acclimatization, not a guarantee that mild pressure will eliminate choking in every future situation.
Study instruction:
- Create three progressively more demanding rehearsal conditions and specify the performance standard required before moving to the next level.
Key point:
- Controlled exposure can build familiarity with relevant pressure cues.
- Increase difficulty gradually and monitor both performance and well-being.
Source links:
- Oudejans: Reality-Based Practice Under Pressure
- Beilock et al.: Choking Under Pressure—Multiple Routes to Skill Failure
33. Q: What did the fMRI study of professional golfers suggest about expert performance under pressure?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a study in which professional and less-experienced golfers imagined a golf shot while their brain activity was measured using functional MRI. The professionals showed a more focused pattern of activity in regions associated with planning and executing the action, while less-experienced golfers showed broader activation that included areas associated with fear and anxiety. The researchers interpreted the pattern as consistent with experts having a more efficient, task-focused organization during motor planning. This is evidence about neural activity during imagined performance, not proof that experts never feel anxiety or that one brain scan can predict every person's performance. The practical lesson is that extensive practice may support stable, task-relevant attention rather than conscious monitoring of every movement.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish what the fMRI study measured from what it did not measure, and explain why brain-activation findings should not be overgeneralized.
Key point:
- Expert motor planning may involve a more focused task-related activation pattern.
- Imaging findings support interpretation but do not establish a universal performance rule.
Source links:
- Milton et al.: The Mind of Expert Motor Performance Is Cool and Focused
34. Q: Why can practicing in front of an audience improve performance during a public performance?
A: Show answer
An audience introduces a relevant social-evaluation cue that may be absent when practicing alone. If a person rehearses only in private, the first experience of being watched may occur during the actual presentation, audition, or competition. Practicing in front of a small audience allows the performer to become familiar with observation, interruptions, and the feeling of being evaluated while still having an opportunity to learn. The chapter describes golf putting practice with observers and suggests similar applications to presentations and client pitches. The audience should be introduced in a supportive, controlled way, with feedback focused on observable performance rather than humiliation. Rehearsal should also include realistic questions and recovery after mistakes.
Study instruction:
- Deliver a short presentation to a small audience, record the questions and distractions, and repeat the presentation after targeted practice.
Key point:
- Rehearse the social conditions that will be present during the real performance.
- Feedback and repetition should build skill rather than intensify fear of evaluation.
Source links:
- Milton et al.: The Mind of Expert Motor Performance Is Cool and Focused
- Oudejans: Reality-Based Practice Under Pressure
35. Q: How should a team prepare for a high-stakes client presentation?
A: Show answer
Preparation should include mastery of the presentation content and rehearsal of the conditions that make the meeting difficult. Team members should practice delivering key messages within the available time, answering unexpected questions, explaining assumptions, handling objections, and acknowledging when information must be checked rather than improvising an unsupported answer. Rehearsals can gradually add observers, senior reviewers, time constraints, and realistic client questions. The team should agree on who handles each topic, how to pass questions between speakers, and how to recover if technology fails or a question falls outside the team's knowledge. A final review should focus on the quality of reasoning and coordination, not merely on memorizing slides.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a mock client meeting with a timed presentation, challenging questions, and a documented review of response quality and coordination.
Key point:
- Rehearse the actual cognitive and social demands of the client meeting.
- Prepare truthful ways to handle uncertainty instead of guessing under pressure.
Source links:
- Oudejans: Reality-Based Practice Under Pressure
- Beilock et al.: Choking Under Pressure—Multiple Routes to Skill Failure
36. Q: How can pressure practice be introduced without creating unnecessary risk or overwhelming learners?
A: Show answer
Begin with a clear learning objective and confirm that participants can perform the basic task safely. Add one stressor at a time, such as an observer, a time limit, or an unfamiliar question, and monitor accuracy, errors, emotional strain, and recovery. Explain that the exercise is practice rather than a judgment of personal worth. Provide a debrief that identifies what worked, what failed, and what should change in the next attempt. Stop or reduce intensity when safety, well-being, or learning quality is compromised. In high-risk settings such as policing, healthcare, or emergency response, simulations require qualified supervision, appropriate safeguards, and compliance with professional standards. The goal is realistic preparation, not shock exposure.
Study instruction:
- Write a safety and progression checklist for a pressure-training exercise, including entry criteria, stop conditions, supervision, and debrief questions.
Key point:
- Effective pressure practice is progressive, task-relevant, and safeguarded.
- More stress is not automatically better training.
Source links:
- Oudejans: Reality-Based Practice Under Pressure
37. Q: Why should high-stakes test results not be treated as a complete measure of someone's ability?
A: Show answer
A high-stakes result reflects performance under a particular set of conditions, not ability in isolation. Anxiety, unfamiliarity, time pressure, task format, preparation, sleep, motivation, and the degree to which the task depends on working memory can all affect the result. The chapter's mathematics findings show that some high-capacity students can lose their usual advantage when pressure disrupts the strategy they use. This does not mean that assessment results are meaningless; it means they should be interpreted alongside other evidence such as prior work, repeated performance, learning history, and the conditions under which the assessment occurred. Where decisions are consequential, assessors should use reliable methods and avoid drawing broad conclusions from one unusually stressful event.
Study instruction:
- Review an assessment decision and identify what additional evidence would help distinguish underlying capability from pressure-related performance variation.
Key point:
- A single pressured performance is an incomplete measure of a person's potential.
- Interpret results in context and use multiple relevant sources of evidence.
Source links:
- Beilock and DeCaro: Working Memory and Problem Solving Under Pressure
- Beilock and Carr: When High-Powered People Fail
38. Q: What can leaders do to reduce the effects of the curse of expertise in their teams?
A: Show answer
Leaders can ask the people who will perform the work to identify likely obstacles before committing to deadlines or promising outcomes. They can involve intermediate users, pilot a process with beginners, review actual task data, and encourage employees to explain where an estimate may be optimistic. Leaders should also create a climate in which raising a realistic concern is not treated as resistance or incompetence. When an employee reports a problem, the manager should ask for evidence, understand the dependency, and revise the plan when warranted. This approach improves estimation and can reveal design or training problems that experts have overlooked. It also strengthens trust because employees see that their operational knowledge influences decisions.
Study instruction:
- Review one team planning process and identify where novice, intermediate, and expert perspectives should be gathered before commitments are approved.
Key point:
- Leaders need input from the people closest to the actual work.
- Accurate planning requires psychological openness as well as technical expertise.
Source links:
- Hinds: The Curse of Expertise
39. Q: How can organizations use pilot testing to reveal hidden novice difficulties?
A: Show answer
A pilot test asks representative first-time or low-experience users to complete a process before it is deployed widely. Observers record completion time, errors, questions, points of hesitation, workarounds, and requests for assistance. The pilot should use realistic instructions, tools, and constraints rather than coaching users through every step. Findings can then inform interface changes, training materials, staffing assumptions, and project schedules. Pilot results should be interpreted carefully: a small or unrepresentative sample may not capture all user needs, and a single successful test does not prove a process is robust. Repeating tests with different users and measuring whether corrections improve performance provides stronger evidence than expert review alone.
Study instruction:
- Design a pilot-test record with participant experience level, task steps, time, errors, questions, and proposed process changes.
Key point:
- Pilot testing exposes friction that experts may not notice.
- Use representative users and repeated evidence before making broad claims about usability.
Source links:
- Hinds: The Curse of Expertise
40. Q: How should a team review a failure that occurred during an important performance?
A: Show answer
A useful review separates the outcome from the process that produced it. The team should reconstruct the task conditions, identify the knowledge and resources available, note the pressure cues, and determine whether the failure involved a skill gap, a planning error, distraction, an unsuitable strategy, or excessive monitoring. The review should also examine whether the rehearsal conditions matched the real event and whether responsibilities or assumptions were unclear. Avoid labeling a person a “choker” or assuming that one mistake reveals a permanent trait. Convert findings into specific practice changes, process improvements, or support needs, then test whether those changes improve performance in a later rehearsal.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a no-blame performance review using evidence about task conditions, decision points, pressure cues, and corrective actions.
Key point:
- Diagnose the mechanism of failure before selecting a remedy.
- Use review findings to improve systems and practice, not to stigmatize individuals.
Source links:
- Beilock et al.: Choking Under Pressure—Multiple Routes to Skill Failure
41. Q: How can the chapter's principles be applied to a student who performs well on practice tests but poorly in the final examination?
A: Show answer
First compare the practice and examination conditions: time limits, room, supervision, question format, consequences, and opportunities to review work. Then determine whether the student struggles with content knowledge, working-memory demands, distracting worry, unfamiliar question types, or a strategy that becomes unreliable under time pressure. Practice should include timed questions, realistic exam conditions, and a deliberate method for reading the question, selecting an approach, and checking the result. The student should also practice recovering after an uncertain question rather than allowing one difficult item to dominate attention. If distress is persistent or severe, appropriate academic or professional support may be useful. The goal is to improve both subject mastery and performance under the actual assessment conditions.
Study instruction:
- Compare one practice test with one simulated examination and record differences in accuracy, time use, strategy, and attention.
Key point:
- Reproduce relevant exam conditions during preparation and identify the source of the performance gap.
- Improve the strategy, not merely the number of practice questions.
Source links:
- Beilock and DeCaro: Working Memory and Problem Solving Under Pressure
- Conway et al.: Working Memory Span Tasks—A Methodological Review
42. Q: What integrated approach does Chapter 1 recommend for overcoming the curse of expertise and improving performance under pressure?
A: Show answer
The chapter brings together two related lessons. First, experts should not assume that their fluency represents the beginner's experience: involve the people doing the work, observe novice performance, test estimates, and make hidden steps visible. Second, high skill does not guarantee reliable performance when the stakes rise: understand whether the task relies on working memory or procedural execution, identify the likely pressure mechanism, and rehearse the relevant conditions. For demanding reasoning, pause to frame the problem and protect attention; for well-learned motor skills, preserve the routine rather than over-monitoring each movement. Use realistic but safe pressure practice, structured debriefs, and repeated evidence to improve. The central message is that expertise must be combined with perspective-taking, task analysis, and deliberate preparation.
Study instruction:
- Create a personal action plan covering novice-perspective checks, estimation calibration, task-specific pressure analysis, rehearsal, and post-performance review.
Key point:
- Expertise becomes more useful when paired with humility about others' learning and one's own limits under pressure.
- Calibrate estimates, match strategies to task demands, and practice realistically.
Source links:
- Hinds: The Curse of Expertise
- Beilock and DeCaro: Working Memory and Problem Solving Under Pressure
- Oudejans: Reality-Based Practice Under Pressure
