Table 3
Working Memory, Creativity, Cognitive Flexibility, and Practice
1. Q: What is the chapter’s central argument about cognitive horsepower?
A: Show answer
Cognitive horsepower—especially working-memory and the ability to control attention—is useful for many tasks, but more conscious control is not always better. Strong focus can help people hold information, ignore distractions, reason, and solve structured problems. The same focus can also lock them into familiar interpretations, suppress unexpected information, or make them overcomplicate a problem. Creative insight, flexible learning, and fluent performance may improve when people loosen deliberate control. The chapter’s central message is therefore not to seek less intelligence, but to learn when to engage analytical control and when to let broader, less constrained processing operate.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter’s contrast between focused reasoning and flexible thinking, then identify one task for each mode.
Key point:
- Working-memory strength is context-dependent: useful for deliberate control, sometimes limiting for creativity and flexibility.
- Use working-memory deliberately rather than maximizing conscious control in every situation.
2. Q: What is working-memory, and how is it different from simply storing information?
A: Show answer
Working-memory is the ability to hold information temporarily in mind while actively manipulating it, updating it, or using it to perform another task. It is more than passive storage: a person must maintain relevant details while reasoning, comparing, planning, or responding to new information. For example, remembering several numbers while calculating a result requires maintaining and updating those numbers as the calculation proceeds. Attention helps working-memory by selecting what should remain available and suppressing competing information. This makes working-memory valuable for complex tasks, but its control processes can also narrow attention too strongly.
Study instruction:
- Explain working-memory using an example that requires holding information and manipulating it at the same time.
Key point:
- Working-memory combines temporary maintenance with active processing and attention control.
- Describe a task that requires updating information, not merely remembering it.
3. Q: Why does the chapter associate working-memory with the prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes the prefrontal cortex as a major brain region involved in working-memory, attention control, and maintaining task goals. Its activity helps people hold information in mind, select what matters, and resist distractions or irrelevant responses. This executive control supports demanding reasoning, but it can also reinforce expectations and the normal interpretation of objects or situations. When people rely too heavily on top-down control, they may miss unusual details or alternative possibilities that other brain systems could detect. The chapter therefore treats prefrontal control as powerful but not universally advantageous.
Study instruction:
- Review the chapter’s explanation of the prefrontal cortex and identify a task where executive control helps and one where it may constrain perception or creativity.
Key point:
- Prefrontal control supports goal-directed thinking but can narrow the search for alternatives.
- Explain both benefits and possible costs of strong executive control.
4. Q: How do the two mammal logic puzzles distinguish logical validity from believability?
A: Show answer
In the chapter’s examples, both arguments use the premises “All mammals can walk” and “Dogs are mammals” or “Dolphins are mammals.” If the premises are accepted, both conclusions logically follow—even though the dolphin conclusion conflicts with real-world knowledge. The task requires judging whether the conclusion follows from the premises, not whether it is factually believable. The second problem is harder for some people because they must inhibit knowledge that dolphins cannot walk. It illustrates how reasoning can be affected by the tendency to substitute truth or familiarity for the requested logical test.
Study instruction:
- Solve both syllogisms using only their stated premises; afterward, separately evaluate whether the premises are true in the real world.
Key point:
- Logical validity asks whether the conclusion follows from premises, not whether the conclusion is believable.
- Separate the logical structure of an argument from your knowledge about reality.
5. Q: When can high working-memory capacity improve performance?
A: Show answer
Higher working-memory capacity can be advantageous when a task requires sustained attention to relevant information, active updating, mental calculation, comprehension, or resisting distractions. The chapter notes associations between working-memory capacity and academic performance, including reading comprehension and mathematical problem-solving. In a logic task, it can help someone keep the premises in mind while suppressing irrelevant beliefs. In a complex calculation, maintaining intermediate values reduces the chance of losing track. These benefits do not imply that high capacity guarantees success; task demands, strategy, knowledge, practice, and stress also affect performance.
Study instruction:
- List three task demands that benefit from working-memory and give a concrete example of each.
Key point:
- Working-memory can improve performance when active maintenance, updating, and selective control are central.
- Identify the task requirement before assuming more working-memory is advantageous.
6. Q: What is a mental set, and how can domain knowledge create it?
A: Show answer
A mental set is a tendency to approach a problem through a familiar strategy, interpretation, or pattern even when a better alternative is available. Domain knowledge can make a particular solution especially accessible, so a person repeatedly sees the problem through the lens of expertise. The chapter’s baseball word-association example shows knowledgeable fans becoming fixated on a baseball-related response instead of a less obvious answer. Expertise supplies useful information, but it can also bias the search toward familiar associations and make it harder to escape a well-practiced frame.
Study instruction:
- Describe a problem in which a familiar professional method could block an unconventional but simpler solution.
Key point:
- Knowledge improves performance until familiarity restricts the search for alternatives.
- Notice when expertise is directing the search too narrowly.
7. Q: Why did baseball knowledge interfere with solving the word-association problem?
A: Show answer
Participants were asked to find a word that could form a compound with “plate,” “broken,” and “shot.” Baseball fans often responded “home,” because home plate is familiar, but it does not fit the other two expressions. “Glass” works as glass plate, broken glass, and shot glass. The chapter reports that baseball fans with higher working-memory were particularly likely to stay with the baseball association. Their ability to focus and retrieve domain-relevant knowledge became a liability because it reinforced the wrong solution. The task illustrates how the first plausible association can anchor thinking and prevent broader search.
Study instruction:
- Work through the three word pairs and explain why the familiar baseball response fails while “glass” satisfies all of them.
Key point:
- The strongest familiar association may be an incorrect mental anchor.
- Test a candidate solution against every constraint rather than accepting its familiarity.
8. Q: What does the candle-box problem demonstrate about functional fixedness?
A: Show answer
The candle-box problem asks a person to attach a candle to a wall using a candle, matches, and a box of tacks. The solution requires emptying the box, using it as a shelf or support, attaching it to the wall, and placing the candle on it. Functional fixedness is the tendency to see an object only in its usual role—in this case, a box as a container for tacks. Creative problem-solving requires re-representing the object by its physical properties and possible uses rather than by its conventional purpose.
Study instruction:
- Draw the candle problem and write down three possible functions for each available object before attempting a solution.
Key point:
- Objects can be reinterpreted as tools, supports, weights, or materials rather than only by their conventional use.
- Separate an object’s physical affordances from its usual function.
9. Q: Why might children sometimes outperform adults on creative insight problems?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes children as less constrained by developed working-memory control and accumulated knowledge of how objects normally work. On some insight tasks, this can make unusual solutions more accessible. The candle-box example suggests that five-year-olds may be less likely to treat the tack box exclusively as a container. This is not a claim that children are generally better problem-solvers or that cognitive development is disadvantageous. It is a task-specific illustration that fewer entrenched assumptions can sometimes make creative restructuring easier.
Study instruction:
- Compare how a novice and an experienced adult might describe the possible uses of an unfamiliar object.
Key point:
- Less-developed assumptions can sometimes support creative re-interpretation, even though mature cognition helps many other tasks.
- Do not generalize one creative advantage into a claim that children outperform adults overall.
10. Q: What does the MacGyver example illustrate about avoiding functional fixedness?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the television character MacGyver to illustrate improvised problem-solving: treating ordinary objects as materials with properties that can be repurposed. The emphasis is on escaping the conventional function of an item and asking what its shape, strength, flexibility, conductivity, or other properties might allow. The example is a memorable illustration rather than scientific proof. In real work, improvisation must also consider safety, reliability, and whether the improvised solution is appropriate for the stakes.
Study instruction:
- Choose an everyday object and identify at least four nonstandard uses based on its physical properties.
Key point:
- Creative improvisation begins by reimagining what available materials can do.
- Repurpose objects thoughtfully, but check safety and reliability before using an improvised solution.
11. Q: How did the Apollo 13 response demonstrate creative problem-solving under constraints?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes the mission’s damaged command module and the need to keep astronauts alive in the lunar module despite incompatible carbon-dioxide canisters. Engineers on the ground devised a way to connect the available command-module canisters to the lunar-module system using materials such as plastic bags, cardboard, and duct tape. The solution required reframing the problem, examining available materials, and designing around a constraint rather than waiting for ideal equipment. The story illustrates practical creativity under pressure, while also showing why teamwork, technical knowledge, testing, and safety are essential.
Study instruction:
- Explain the constraint in the Apollo 13 example and identify how the engineers changed the way they represented the available materials.
Key point:
- Creative solutions often emerge when a problem is reframed around constraints and available resources.
- When resources are limited, list constraints and materials before rejecting unconventional options.
12. Q: How does the chapter’s string-and-screwdriver puzzle reveal differences in problem-solving strategy?
A: Show answer
The puzzle involves two ceiling strings too far apart to reach simultaneously, with a screwdriver, matches, and cotton available. One solution uses the screwdriver as a pendulum weight: swing one string, then catch it while holding the other. A child suggested simply standing on the table to reach both strings. The contrast shows that people can search for clever, complex strategies while overlooking a direct solution that changes the assumed physical constraints. The point is not that the pendulum idea is wrong, but that a simpler possibility should be tested first.
Study instruction:
- Recreate the string problem and list the simplest physical change that would allow both strings to be reached.
Key point:
- Creative ingenuity is useful, but a basic change in perspective may solve the problem more simply.
- Before building a complex workaround, test whether the task’s assumptions can be changed.
13. Q: Why can high cognitive horsepower lead to unnecessarily complex solutions?
A: Show answer
Strong working-memory and executive control may encourage people to construct detailed, multi-step strategies and stay committed to them. When a problem becomes familiar, the solver may keep using a complex procedure even after a simpler option appears. The chapter’s string puzzle and water-jug experiment illustrate this tendency. Complexity can consume time, create more opportunities for error, and obscure the actual objective. A good solver therefore compares alternatives and checks whether the simplest valid method satisfies the constraints rather than equating sophistication with effectiveness.
Study instruction:
- Take a complex solution from a work or study task and see whether a shorter method achieves the same result without losing accuracy.
Key point:
- More steps do not necessarily mean better reasoning; unnecessary complexity can reduce efficiency.
- Evaluate solutions by correctness, simplicity, and fit to the task—not by how elaborate they are.
14. Q: What was the Luchins water-jug task, and what did it test?
A: Show answer
The Luchins water-jug task presents containers with different capacities and asks participants to measure a target quantity using a specified strategy. In the chapter’s study, participants first encountered problems that required a difficult multistep formula, then later problems that could be solved more simply. The researchers were interested in whether people would notice and use the simpler strategy when it became available. The task therefore tests persistence with an established method, strategy flexibility, and sensitivity to a simpler alternative—not merely arithmetic ability.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a sequence of problems can create a learned strategy and then test whether the solver can abandon it.
Key point:
- A learned strategy can become a mental set that persists after the problem changes.
- Look for a change in constraints that makes a previously learned method unnecessary.
15. Q: Why did the water-jug study ask participants to use the simplest strategy possible?
A: Show answer
The instruction to use the simplest strategy made strategy selection an explicit part of the task. Participants had to do more than produce the correct quantity; they were expected to compare methods and choose an economical one. The chapter describes early problems that required a multistep formula, followed by a problem solvable with a simpler subtraction method. Without the simplicity constraint, a solver could continue using the earlier formula and still get the right answer, hiding the failure to adapt. The study therefore examined whether people recognized and switched to a more efficient method.
Study instruction:
- Identify the objective and constraints of a problem before solving it, then compare the method you used with simpler valid alternatives.
Key point:
- The most efficient strategy is not always the one most recently learned.
- Check for simpler methods whenever the task changes or new information appears.
16. Q: What is strategy fixation, and how can a solver recognize it?
A: Show answer
Strategy fixation occurs when a person continues using a previously successful approach even though the current problem permits or requires a different method. Warning signs include repeating the same steps, ignoring a new constraint, overlooking shortcuts, or treating a familiar formula as mandatory. The chapter’s water-jug results suggest that higher-working-memory students may be more likely to persist with a difficult method. Fixation is not always obvious because the repeated strategy may still produce a correct answer; inefficiency and failure to explore alternatives are the clues.
Study instruction:
- After solving a problem, identify the assumptions embedded in your chosen method and test whether any are unnecessary.
Key point:
- A correct answer can still reveal an inflexible or inefficient strategy.
- When a method feels automatic, deliberately search for an alternative before committing.
17. Q: How can narrow attention be helpful and harmful at the same time?
A: Show answer
Narrow attention helps people select relevant information and ignore distractions, which can improve demanding tasks such as logical reasoning, mental calculation, or maintaining a goal. However, a narrow attentional spotlight can suppress awareness of information outside the focus. When a problem requires noticing unexpected cues, discovering a new relationship, or exploring alternatives, the same control can become restrictive. The chapter emphasizes that attention is not simply good or bad; its value depends on whether the task rewards concentration or broad awareness.
Study instruction:
- For two tasks—one requiring precision and one requiring discovery—decide whether focused or broad attention is more appropriate and justify the choice.
Key point:
- Selective attention supports precision but may reduce awareness of unexpected information.
- Match attentional breadth to the task rather than maintaining one mode constantly.
18. Q: What does the Stanford–California “Big Game” play illustrate about missing unexpected events?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts the 1982 Stanford–California football game in which California returned a kickoff through multiple lateral passes while the Stanford band had already entered the field. The band members appeared to be focused on the expected end of the game and failed to notice the unfolding play. The story illustrates how strong expectations and attention to a planned outcome can reduce responsiveness to surprising events. It is an anecdote used to explain attentional limitations, not proof that working-memory capacity alone caused any individual to miss the play.
Study instruction:
- Describe the expected event and the unexpected event in the story, then identify what attention may have filtered out.
Key point:
- Expectation-driven focus can make people overlook important events that do not fit their current model.
- During critical situations, periodically scan for cues that contradict the expected outcome.
19. Q: What is the cocktail-party effect, and how does the chapter connect it with working-memory?
A: Show answer
The cocktail-party effect refers to the ability to detect personally relevant information—such as hearing one’s name—in a conversation or noisy environment while attention is directed elsewhere. The chapter reports that people lower in working-memory may show stronger detection of such unexpected information because their attention is less tightly focused on one stream. The effect illustrates a trade-off: broad awareness can reveal relevant cues, but it may also make sustained concentration harder. It does not mean that distraction is always desirable or that lower working-memory is superior.
Study instruction:
- Explain how hearing your name in a conversation you are not following illustrates selective attention and unexpected cue detection.
Key point:
- Broad attention can help detect personally meaningful information outside the main focus.
- Recognize the value of peripheral awareness when the environment may contain unexpected cues.
20. Q: Why might broad awareness be useful for creativity, language, or unexpected-event detection?
A: Show answer
Broad awareness allows more information to enter the processing stream instead of being tightly filtered to the current goal. That can help people notice weak signals, unusual associations, or details that seem unrelated but later become useful. The chapter connects this mode with creative problem-solving, language learning, and detecting unexpected events. Broad attention is not a replacement for concentration: it can increase distraction and make tasks requiring precise control harder. The useful skill is to shift between exploratory awareness and focused evaluation.
Study instruction:
- During a brainstorming session, deliberately notice unrelated details and then evaluate whether any create a useful connection to the problem.
Key point:
- Exploration benefits from a wider search, while evaluation often benefits from focused attention.
- Use broad attention to generate possibilities and narrow attention to test them.
21. Q: Why can children be especially effective at acquiring a second language?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that children’s developing working-memory and executive-control systems may make them less likely to impose adult-style chunks or expectations on language input. They can attend to smaller parts of speech and learn patterns from exposure, while adults may rely on familiar word combinations and prior rules. This is one explanation for children’s language-learning advantage, not the only one; exposure, social interaction, practice, and developmental factors also matter. The chapter uses the idea to show that less top-down control can sometimes facilitate learning.
Study instruction:
- Compare how a child and an adult might notice unfamiliar word endings and sentence patterns during language exposure.
Key point:
- Less rigid processing may help learners discover linguistic patterns from individual components.
- Consider whether prior knowledge is helping or prematurely shaping what you hear.
22. Q: How can adults’ tendency to process familiar word chunks interfere with language learning?
A: Show answer
Adults often recognize frequent letter, word, or phrase combinations as familiar units. This efficient chunking normally helps comprehension, but it can interfere when the new language uses those components differently. The chapter describes adults carrying a familiar plural ending into a context where a singular form is required. Because the whole chunk is strongly represented, the learner may fail to notice the smaller grammatical pieces. The problem is not that chunking is inherently bad; it is that a familiar pattern may be applied before the learner has accurately identified the new rule.
Study instruction:
- Take an unfamiliar language example and identify its individual sounds, endings, and word components before treating the phrase as a single unit.
Key point:
- Automatic chunk recognition can help fluency but also carry old patterns into new learning.
- Slow down and inspect components when a new system does not follow familiar rules.
23. Q: What did the artificial-language study by Kersten and Earles suggest about adult learning?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that adults learned word meanings and rules in a miniature artificial language more effectively when they first encountered individual words and later more complex sentences. Beginning with full complexity encouraged adults to process familiar combinations as chunks, whereas staged exposure made it easier to learn individual components. The implication is that training design can change the cognitive demands of learning. It does not prove that every subject should always be taught in fragments; sequence and complexity should match the learning goal and learner.
Study instruction:
- Design a two-stage language lesson: introduce components first, then combine them in increasingly complex sentences.
Key point:
- Reducing initial complexity can help adults learn components before they must manage them together.
- Sequence learning from simple elements to combinations when unfamiliar patterns are difficult.
24. Q: How should a learner decide when working-memory is an advantage versus a disadvantage?
A: Show answer
Working-memory is an advantage when the task depends on holding information, following several steps, comparing options, or resisting distraction. It may be a disadvantage when success requires loosened associations, reinterpreting familiar objects, detecting peripheral information, or allowing automatic skills to run without excessive conscious monitoring. The chapter’s examples span logic, mathematics, creativity, language, and sport. The practical decision is to diagnose the task: does it reward deliberate control and precision, or flexible exploration and fluency?
Study instruction:
- Sort several tasks into deliberate-control and flexible-exploration categories, then explain why the attention mode fits each task.
Key point:
- The same cognitive ability can support or hinder performance depending on task demands.
- Choose a mental strategy for the task instead of treating one cognitive mode as universally best.
25. Q: What does the chapter mean by having cognitive horsepower available but being able to turn it down?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s ideal is flexible control: use working-memory and deliberate attention when the task requires them, but reduce overcontrol when they interfere with creativity, language learning, or practiced performance. Turning it down does not mean becoming careless or abandoning standards. It means avoiding unnecessary conscious monitoring, allowing other cues and associations to contribute, and not forcing a familiar strategy onto every situation. This is a metacognitive skill—recognizing when the current mode of thinking is helping and when it is constraining the task.
Study instruction:
- Identify a task where you tend to overthink and choose one method for reducing unnecessary conscious control without sacrificing safety or accuracy.
Key point:
- Effective performance depends on flexible use of cognitive control, not constant maximum effort.
- Practice switching between deliberate analysis and relaxed, exploratory processing.
26. Q: What did the prefrontal-cortex injury matchstick study suggest about insight?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes researchers comparing healthy adults with people who had lateral prefrontal-cortex damage on unusual mathematical matchstick problems. Both groups performed similarly on straightforward problems, but on a problem requiring an unconventional reinterpretation, the patients performed better: the chapter reports 82 percent correct for the patient group versus 43 percent for healthy adults. The finding suggests that strong executive constraints can sometimes interfere with insight. It does not mean brain injury is beneficial overall; the study isolates a narrow task and a specific cognitive trade-off.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a task that requires changing how a symbol is interpreted may challenge habitual control more than a routine arithmetic task.
Key point:
- Relaxing habitual constraints can sometimes make unusual representations easier to see.
- Treat the study as evidence of a task-specific trade-off, not as a general benefit of brain injury.
27. Q: What is the difference between routine problem-solving and insight problem-solving?
A: Show answer
Routine problems can often be solved by applying a known procedure to familiar inputs. Insight problems require restructuring the representation of the problem, changing an assumption, or noticing an overlooked possibility. The chapter’s matchstick example illustrates that a person may understand arithmetic but still fail to see that a symbol can be altered in an unconventional way. Insight often involves escaping the constraints that were assumed rather than adding more steps. A strong routine solver may need to deliberately question the representation before trying another calculation.
Study instruction:
- Choose one familiar problem and write down its assumed rules; then test whether any rule is merely habitual rather than necessary.
Key point:
- Insight may require changing the representation, not increasing the number of calculations.
- When stuck, question the problem’s framing and hidden assumptions.
28. Q: How did distraction improve learning in the chapter’s American Sign Language experiment?
A: Show answer
In the reported experiment, college students learned simple ASL sentences through video. One group watched without an additional task; another counted high-pitched tones while learning. The distracted group later performed better when producing new sentences from learned signs. The chapter interprets the distraction as reducing the ability to hold entire sentences as chunks, encouraging learning of individual signs that could be recombined flexibly. This result concerns a specific experimental setup and should not be taken as a general recommendation to distract learners during all instruction.
Study instruction:
- Explain what the tone-counting task changed during learning and why that could improve recombination of signs.
Key point:
- Dividing working-memory in a controlled way may encourage component learning and flexible transfer.
- Distinguish a targeted learning experiment from a blanket rule that distraction improves learning.
29. Q: Why is generalization more important than reproducing a memorized example in language learning?
A: Show answer
Generalization means using learned components or rules in new combinations rather than merely repeating sentences that were practiced. In the ASL study, the outcome of interest included producing novel sentences from familiar signs. The chapter argues that learning individual elements may support this flexible recombination better than memorizing whole sentence patterns. Generalization shows whether the learner has acquired a usable structure, although it should be assessed with tasks that genuinely differ from the practice examples.
Study instruction:
- Create new sentence combinations using familiar vocabulary or signs and check whether the combinations follow the learned rules.
Key point:
- Transfer to new combinations indicates flexible learning beyond exact memorization.
- Test learning with novel examples, not only repeated practice items.
30. Q: How can deliberate distraction reduce overthinking in a practiced motor skill?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that experienced golfers were more likely to hole a short putt when given a secondary task that occupied working-memory, such as counting backward by threes or singing. A highly practiced movement can be disrupted when the performer consciously monitors each component. A secondary task may reduce excessive self-focus and allow the motor routine to execute more fluently. The technique is task-specific and should not be used when divided attention creates danger or when the skill still requires conscious control.
Study instruction:
- Describe a safe, well-practiced skill and explain how a simple secondary task might reduce intrusive monitoring; identify when distraction would be unsafe.
Key point:
- Conscious control can disrupt automatic performance when a skill is already well learned.
- Use reduced self-monitoring only for stable, practiced tasks and never when safety or decision-making requires full attention.
31. Q: Why might deliberate attention to every step harm an expert’s performance?
A: Show answer
After practice, a skill may become coordinated and automatic. Attempting to consciously control every movement can introduce hesitation, disrupt timing, or make the performer focus on components that normally operate smoothly. The chapter uses skilled golf putting to illustrate this effect. The risk is greatest when a person is already competent and the task is relatively simple; deliberate attention remains useful for learning, correcting errors, or adapting to changed conditions. The goal is to avoid unnecessary control rather than stop monitoring outcomes.
Study instruction:
- Compare how you would perform a new skill versus a familiar skill and describe where conscious monitoring belongs in each.
Key point:
- Automatic routines may be more reliable than step-by-step conscious control after extensive practice.
- Let practiced execution run, while keeping attention on the overall goal and feedback.
32. Q: How does REM sleep relate to creativity and making connections?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes REM sleep as involving reduced prefrontal activation and greater activity in some sensory brain regions. It cites research suggesting that people may become better at connecting seemingly unrelated information after REM sleep. The proposed explanation is that reduced executive control can allow associations to emerge that were not obvious during tightly focused waking thought. The chapter does not suggest that sleep replaces knowledge or deliberate analysis; rather, sleep may support memory processing and flexible connection-making.
Study instruction:
- After studying a complex topic, record unresolved questions and revisit them after adequate sleep; note any new connections without treating them as automatically correct.
Key point:
- Rest and reduced executive control may help some forms of associative insight.
- Use sleep and incubation as complements to deliberate study, then verify any insight with evidence.
33. Q: How can pressure cause working-memory to be used inefficiently?
A: Show answer
The chapter introduces choking under pressure as a problem of using working-memory in the wrong way—either devoting excessive attention to a well-practiced action or failing to allocate enough cognitive resources to a demanding task. Anxiety and self-monitoring can consume resources needed for execution, while insufficient attention can lead to missed information. Performance therefore depends on matching attention to task requirements and managing pressure, not simply trying harder or concentrating more intensely.
Study instruction:
- Identify whether a pressured task requires careful analysis or automatic execution and plan a matching attention strategy.
Key point:
- Pressure can distort the allocation of working-memory and conscious control.
- Before a high-stakes task, decide what to monitor deliberately and what to leave to practiced routine.
34. Q: What does the twin research described in the chapter indicate about genetic influences on working-memory?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes research comparing identical twins, who share nearly all their genes, with fraternal twins, who share about half on average. Twins completed tasks including letter updating, which requires keeping the most recent letters in mind as new letters arrive. Identical twins were more similar on these measures than fraternal twins, supporting a genetic contribution to differences in working-memory and cognitive ability. Twin similarity is evidence about population variation, not a precise prediction of any individual’s potential or destiny.
Study instruction:
- Explain why comparing identical and fraternal twins raised together can help researchers estimate genetic contributions to cognitive differences.
Key point:
- Greater similarity among identical twins is consistent with genetic influence on cognitive traits.
- Interpret twin findings as population-level evidence, not as a deterministic forecast for an individual.
35. Q: What is the letter-updating task, and why does it measure working-memory?
A: Show answer
In the letter-updating task, letters appear one at a time and participants must continuously report the most recent three letters in their correct order. As each new letter arrives, the oldest letter is discarded and the new one added. This requires attention to incoming information, maintaining a short sequence, updating it, and preventing confusion from earlier letters. The task is therefore a dynamic measure of active memory rather than simple recall of a fixed list.
Study instruction:
- Practice the updating sequence using a stream of letters and explain at each step which letter is removed and which is added.
Key point:
- Working-memory requires continuous updating as relevant information changes.
- Focus on maintaining the current information set and replacing outdated items.
36. Q: Why is working-memory often associated with general intellectual ability?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that working-memory supports the ability to hold relevant information while reasoning and to control attention so that the information is not lost or confused. These processes are involved in many academic tasks, including reading comprehension, mathematical reasoning, and complex problem-solving. The chapter cites estimates that working-memory differences account for a substantial proportion of variation in general intellectual ability. Such estimates depend on the measures and samples used; working-memory is an important contributor, not a complete explanation of intelligence or achievement.
Study instruction:
- Map a reading, calculation, and planning task to the information each requires you to maintain and update.
Key point:
- Working-memory supports multiple demanding cognitive activities and is related to, but not identical with, general ability.
- Explain the contribution of working-memory without treating it as the sole determinant of intelligence.
37. Q: What role does dopamine and the COMT gene play in the chapter’s discussion of cognitive differences?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses dopamine as a neurotransmitter involved in signaling, memory updating, and attention. It describes variation in the COMT gene, including Val and Met forms, which are associated with differences in dopamine metabolism. The author presents these biological differences as one possible contributor to variation in cognitive horsepower. This is not a simple gene-to-performance rule: cognitive outcomes arise from many biological and environmental factors, and the chapter’s discussion should not be used to diagnose ability or predict an individual’s performance from genotype.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the proposed link among dopamine, COMT variation, memory updating, and attention without claiming a deterministic effect.
Key point:
- Biological variation may contribute to cognitive differences, but it does not dictate an individual’s abilities.
- Avoid inferring cognitive performance from a single gene or neurotransmitter pathway.
38. Q: How should genetic and environmental influences on cognitive ability be considered together?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses Sara’s school environment and twin research to show that educational opportunity and genetic variation can both influence cognitive development and outcomes. A strong school environment does not guarantee a particular academic trajectory, and differences in measured ability do not eliminate the value of learning opportunities. The chapter also emphasizes that cognitive capacity can change through training. A balanced interpretation avoids treating genes or environment as mutually exclusive explanations and recognizes that access, motivation, instruction, practice, health, and context shape performance.
Study instruction:
- Draw a two-way model showing how inherited differences, learning opportunities, practice, and task context may interact over time.
Key point:
- Cognitive development reflects interacting influences rather than a single fixed cause.
- Use both biological and environmental explanations, and avoid deterministic conclusions.
39. Q: What evidence in the chapter challenges the idea that working-memory is fixed?
A: Show answer
The chapter summarizes studies in which participants improved working-memory or attention through structured practice. Children with ADHD and typical adults performed progressive memory tasks and showed improvements on trained tasks, with some benefits reported for other attention or reasoning measures. It also discusses attention training in children and video-game studies. These findings support the idea that aspects of cognitive performance can be trainable, but they do not mean every training program produces broad, lasting improvements for everyone.
Study instruction:
- Compare the chapter’s training studies with the fixed-capacity assumption and identify what outcome changed.
Key point:
- Practice can alter some working-memory and attention performance, challenging the idea that cognitive capacity is entirely immutable.
- Evaluate cognitive-training claims by looking at the measured outcome and transfer evidence.
40. Q: How was working-memory training used in the ADHD study described by Klingberg?
A: Show answer
Children diagnosed with ADHD were assigned to a treatment or placebo-style control condition. The treatment involved intensive verbal and spatial working-memory tasks over several weeks, including remembering letters and recalling them in reverse order. Difficulty increased as participants improved, continually challenging their capacity. The comparison group performed similar computer activities but did not receive the same progressive increase in working-memory load. The chapter reports improvements in trained memory tasks and some attention-related measures among the treatment group.
Study instruction:
- Describe the training duration, task type, progression, and comparison condition from the chapter.
Key point:
- Progressively challenging working-memory tasks were used to test whether memory training could improve attention-related outcomes.
- Distinguish the active training from the comparison condition and identify the outcomes measured.
41. Q: Why did the ADHD training program increase task difficulty as children improved?
A: Show answer
Increasing the number or complexity of items kept the training demanding instead of allowing participants to repeat an easy task after mastering it. The chapter describes the program as continually pushing working-memory capacity through adaptive practice. Progression provides a clearer challenge and can prevent training from becoming merely repetitive. However, progression alone does not guarantee meaningful improvement in daily life; the task must be appropriately designed, and outcomes should be measured beyond the practice exercise.
Study instruction:
- Design a progressive practice plan that increases difficulty only after the learner consistently performs the current level well.
Key point:
- Adaptive challenge keeps practice demanding as performance improves.
- Progress difficulty in a controlled way and measure whether improvement extends beyond the training task.
42. Q: What was the significance of the placebo or control group in the ADHD working-memory study?
A: Show answer
The control group helps distinguish the effects of the targeted working-memory training from those of computer use, attention from adults, repeated testing, or expectations. In the chapter’s account, children in the comparison group performed similar activities but did not receive the same adaptive memory challenge. The contrast supports a more credible inference about the training program, although a single study cannot establish that the intervention works for every child or that all symptoms will change. The chapter also notes a double-blind design, reducing expectation-related bias.
Study instruction:
- Identify what the comparison group controls for and why researchers would keep participants and assessors unaware of assignment.
Key point:
- Control conditions help isolate intervention effects from practice, attention, and expectation.
- Look for the control design before attributing an observed improvement to training.
43. Q: What is the difference between improvement on a trained task and transfer to other tasks?
A: Show answer
Improvement on a trained task means participants become better at the specific activity they practiced. Transfer means the improvement also appears in other tasks that use related abilities but were not directly practiced. The chapter reports transfer to some attention and reasoning measures in several training studies, which is more informative than performance gains on the exercise itself. Transfer must still be measured carefully: different tasks may share only part of the underlying skill, and broad claims about school, work, or everyday functioning require evidence on those outcomes.
Study instruction:
- When reviewing a cognitive-training claim, list the trained task and every untrained outcome separately.
Key point:
- Transfer beyond the trained exercise is a stronger test of broader usefulness than task-specific improvement alone.
- Check the scope of transfer before concluding that a training program improves general intelligence.
44. Q: What did Michael Posner’s attention-training work with young children suggest?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes nursery-school children practicing attention-related tasks, including controlling a joystick to guide an animated object and predicting its movement. Exercises became progressively more difficult. The reported training was followed by improvements in practiced tasks and changes in frontal brain activity, including regions involved in attention control and shifting. The chapter suggests that structured attention practice may influence developing control systems. It does not establish that any simple game or brief exercise produces the same developmental effects.
Study instruction:
- Identify the skill practiced in the joystick task, how challenge increased, and which attention functions the exercise targeted.
Key point:
- Structured attention practice may influence task performance and developing attention networks.
- Connect a training exercise to the specific cognitive process it targets and the outcome actually measured.
45. Q: What did adult working-memory training and fMRI findings add to the chapter’s argument?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports adults practicing working-memory tasks over several weeks, such as remembering letters and reproducing them in reverse order. Participants improved on practiced tasks and some attention and reasoning measures. fMRI results showed increased activity in brain regions associated with working-memory, including prefrontal areas, after training. The chapter interprets this as evidence that adult cognitive performance and neural activity can change with practice. It should not be read as proof that every cognitive-training product causes durable brain-wide enhancement.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the behavioral changes and the brain-imaging findings separately, then explain why both measures matter.
Key point:
- The chapter reports behavioral improvement alongside neural activity changes after training.
- Do not equate brain activation changes automatically with broad or permanent cognitive enhancement.
46. Q: How can action video games improve some cognitive skills according to the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes action games as demanding frequent shifts of attention, updating mission goals, monitoring multiple cues, and responding to events while remembering objectives. In a study, novice college students played Medal of Honor for about an hour a day over ten days; the chapter reports improvements in memory and attention, including tasks they had not directly practiced. The proposed benefit is the repeated exercise of cognitive processes under dynamic conditions, not simply entertainment or long hours of gaming.
Study instruction:
- Identify the cognitive demands of the game and compare them with the attention and memory measures used outside the game.
Key point:
- Games can exercise attention and memory when their mechanics repeatedly demand those skills.
- Assess games by their cognitive demands and measured outcomes, not by genre or playtime alone.
47. Q: What was Space Fortress, and how did the chapter describe its transfer to flight performance?
A: Show answer
Space Fortress was a computer-game-style training environment designed to exercise attention, memory, and strategic control while players navigated and responded to multiple demands. The chapter describes Israeli Air Force cadets practicing for roughly ten hours across ten days and reports an improvement of nearly 30 percent in actual flight performance. The example is important because it describes transfer from a game-like task to a real-world skill. It should be treated as a specific research result, not as a universal guarantee that gaming improves performance in every domain.
Study instruction:
- Explain why the reported flight-performance outcome is more meaningful than a score improvement inside Space Fortress alone.
Key point:
- A training simulation is valuable when its practiced skills transfer to the real performance context.
- Separate game performance from the real-world outcome the training is intended to improve.
48. Q: How does the chapter connect ADHD and divergent thinking without treating them as a universal advantage?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses research in which college students with ADHD generated more unusual uses for common objects than students without ADHD. The proposed explanation involves less inhibition of information, allowing more ideas to enter awareness and supporting divergent thinking. This may help certain creative tasks, but ADHD is also associated with difficulties in attention, working-memory, and impulse regulation that can impair other activities. The finding is task-specific and should not be used to romanticize a condition or assume that all individuals with ADHD share the same strengths.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish divergent idea generation from tasks requiring sustained attention, planning, or accuracy.
Key point:
- Reduced filtering may support creative idea generation in some tasks while creating challenges elsewhere.
- Recognize individual variability and the task-specific nature of reported ADHD-related creativity.
49. Q: What ethical caution does the chapter raise about interpreting group differences in cognitive ability?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that claims about average cognitive differences between sexes or racial groups can be used to justify discriminatory educational or employment policies. It also discusses how asserting or emphasizing such differences can create stressful situations that may affect performance. Interpreting group comparisons requires careful attention to measurement, opportunity, context, uncertainty, and individual variation. A group average cannot establish an individual’s ability, worth, or potential. Claims should not be used as a substitute for fair evaluation of people or for addressing unequal conditions.
Study instruction:
- Review an example of a group-comparison claim and identify its measurement limits, environmental factors, and potential consequences if misapplied.
Key point:
- Group statistics cannot justify deterministic judgments about individuals or unequal opportunities.
- Evaluate group-difference claims critically and protect fair, evidence-based individual assessment.
50. Q: How can someone apply the chapter’s ideas to solve a difficult problem more flexibly?
A: Show answer
Start by defining the goal and constraints, then write down the assumptions you are treating as fixed. Generate several representations of the problem: what it looks like mathematically, physically, socially, or from a beginner’s perspective. Identify the first familiar solution and deliberately search for alternatives. Try a simple method before building a complex one; use analogies or unrelated cues to loosen fixation. Once ideas are generated, return to focused evaluation and test each candidate for correctness, feasibility, safety, and evidence.
Study instruction:
- Use this sequence on a real problem: define it, expose assumptions, generate alternatives, test the simplest option, then evaluate.
Key point:
- Flexible problem-solving combines broad exploration with disciplined testing.
- Separate idea generation from verification and test simple alternatives before committing.
51. Q: How can learners design practice that strengthens working-memory or attention without overclaiming?
A: Show answer
Use targeted exercises that require maintaining, updating, or shifting information, gradually increasing difficulty as performance becomes reliable. Include feedback and track accuracy, speed, fatigue, and consistency. Then test skills on unfamiliar tasks that share the relevant cognitive demands, rather than assuming practice automatically transfers. The chapter’s studies support the possibility of training-related change, but outcomes depend on program design, participants, and measures. Training should supplement appropriate educational, clinical, or performance support, not replace it.
Study instruction:
- Create a short practice plan with a specific cognitive skill, progressive challenge, feedback, and an untrained transfer test.
Key point:
- Structured, progressive practice can improve specific abilities, but broader transfer must be demonstrated.
- Define the target skill and evaluate both trained performance and practical transfer.
52. Q: What are the chapter’s most important practical lessons about attention, creativity, and cognitive control?
A: Show answer
The chapter rejects the idea that maximum conscious control is always optimal. Working-memory supports logic, calculation, and focused learning, yet it can also encourage fixation, complexity, and overmonitoring. Creative insight may require broader awareness, reinterpreting objects, or reducing the influence of familiar knowledge. Practice can improve some cognitive functions, and performance may be shaped by genes, environment, and training. The practical lesson is to diagnose the task, switch attention modes intentionally, use structured practice, test assumptions, and verify ideas rather than relying on one preferred style of thinking.
Study instruction:
- Create a personal checklist of when to focus, when to broaden attention, when to reduce overthinking, and how to test the result.
Key point:
- Effective thinking requires flexible control, creativity, practice, and evidence-based evaluation.
- Choose the cognitive mode that fits the task and verify whether it improves actual performance.
