- Shih, Pittinsky and Ambady (1999): Identity Salience and Quantitative Performance
33. Q: How can making one social identity salient affect performance?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes research in which Asian-American women performed differently depending on whether their ethnic identity or gender identity was made salient before a mathematics task. The study reported higher performance when the ethnic identity associated with a positive quantitative stereotype was activated and lower performance when the gender identity associated with a negative stereotype was activated, compared with a control condition. The finding illustrates how context can change which aspect of identity is foremost in a situation. It should not be used to endorse stereotypes or to infer ability from ethnicity or gender. The important lesson is that social cues can influence how people interpret an evaluative task.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the experimental manipulation and why the result must not be interpreted as evidence that group stereotypes are true."
Key point:
- Identity cues can change the meaning of an evaluative situation.
- Stereotypes are social beliefs, not valid measures of an individual’s ability.
Source links:
- Shih, Pittinsky and Ambady (1999): Identity Salience and Quantitative Performance
34. Q: Why can reflecting on several identities be different from emphasizing only one positive identity?
A: Show answer
A single identity can still leave a person feeling defined by one social category or one domain. Reflecting on several meaningful roles can make the self-concept less narrow and reduce the sense that a test or performance determines the person’s entire value. The chapter describes a study in which women asked to consider multiple facets of themselves were less likely to underperform on a high-stakes mathematics test than women who did not complete the exercise. Later research has not shown that every identity-based exercise works consistently, so the technique should be understood as a possible contextual support rather than a universal remedy. The exercise should reflect authentic identities rather than impose categories on the learner.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a single-identity prompt with a multiple-facets prompt and explain which might be more suitable in a particular situation."
Key point:
- Multiple identities can broaden the frame through which a person interprets an evaluation.
- The effectiveness of identity prompts depends on context and evidence.
Source links:
- Shih, Pittinsky and Ambady (1999): Identity Salience and Quantitative Performance
- Liu et al. (2021): Meta-Analysis of Stereotype-Threat Interventions
- Stoevenbelt et al. (2026): Registered Replication Report
35. Q: How can the placement of demographic questions affect a test environment?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses research suggesting that asking about sex or other demographic information before a test may make group identity more salient at a moment when performance is being evaluated. In the applied testing example, moving background questions from the beginning to the end was associated with improved mathematics performance for women in the researchers’ reanalysis. This is a low-cost design consideration, but the evidence has been debated and should not be treated as settled for every test. Test administrators should consider whether demographic information is genuinely needed before the assessment, whether it can be collected afterward, and whether any change affects data quality or fairness.
Study instruction:
- "Review a test registration process and identify which demographic questions are necessary before the assessment and which could be collected afterward."
Key point:
- Assessment procedures can shape the psychological context of a test.
- Administrative changes should be evaluated for both performance and data-quality consequences.
Source links:
- Danaher and Crandall (2008): Stereotype Threat in Applied Settings
- Stoevenbelt et al. (2026): Registered Replication Report
36. Q: How can teaching people about stereotype threat help in some settings?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a study in which women performed differently depending on how a mathematics task was framed and whether participants were informed about stereotype threat. Explaining that anxiety may arise from widely known stereotypes, rather than from a person’s actual ability, was associated with improved performance in that experiment. The rationale is that the person can reinterpret anxiety without treating it as evidence of incompetence. However, a 2026 registered replication of this particular line of research did not find the predicted stereotype-threat effect in nine direct replications. Therefore, education about stereotypes may support awareness and reduce self-blame for some people, but it should not be marketed as a reliably proven test-performance intervention.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the proposed reattribution mechanism and compare the original study with the later registered replication."
Key point:
- Reframing anxiety may help some people avoid interpreting it as proof of low ability.
- Specific intervention claims must be updated when registered replications challenge them.
Source links:
- Johns, Schmader and Martens (2005): Teaching Stereotype Threat
- Stoevenbelt et al. (2026): Registered Replication Report
37. Q: What is the “Obama effect” discussed in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a study that compared verbal-test performance among Black and White participants at several points during the 2008 US presidential campaign. The researchers reported that Black participants’ performance improved around highly salient moments in Barack Obama’s nomination acceptance and election, when a counter-stereotypic role model’s success was prominent. The proposed mechanism is that a visible achievement by an in-group role model can weaken the perceived credibility of a negative group stereotype. The study used repeated groups at different time points rather than randomly assigning people to observe a role model, so time-related factors and other explanations must be considered. It is evidence consistent with a role-model effect, not proof that one public event caused all performance changes.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the timing-based design and identify why it supports an association more strongly than a definitive causal claim."
Key point:
- Visible counter-stereotypic achievement may challenge negative expectations.
- The study’s design requires caution about causal interpretation.
Source links:
- Marx, Ko and Friedman (2009): The Obama Effect
38. Q: What makes a role model potentially useful in a domain where a group is stereotyped negatively?
A: Show answer
A role model can provide a concrete example that the stereotype is not a reliable description of what members of the group can achieve. The chapter notes that role models are most useful when the person is perceived as competent, seen as relevant or relatable to the learner, and visibly successful in the domain where the stereotype is active. A role model can also make an unfamiliar career or pathway seem more attainable by showing the steps, setbacks, and supports involved. However, one exceptional individual cannot prove that structural barriers have disappeared. Role models should expand perceived possibilities while institutions also address unequal access, evaluation practices, and opportunity.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a proposed role model by competence, relevance, visibility of the achievement, and the learner’s opportunity to connect with the example."
Key point:
- Role models can make counter-stereotypic achievement more visible and credible.
- Representation helps but does not replace structural change or equal access.
Source links:
- Marx, Ko and Friedman (2009): The Obama Effect
- Dasgupta and Asgari (2004): Exposure to Counterstereotypic Women Leaders
39. Q: How can exposure to women leaders influence students’ expectations about academic and professional roles?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes research comparing students’ exposure to women in leadership and faculty roles. Students who regularly encountered women leaders were less likely to endorse automatic stereotypes about women’s suitability for leadership than students with less exposure. The chapter also discusses evidence from the US Air Force Academy suggesting that female students’ performance and later academic choices could be influenced by having female professors in introductory mathematics and science courses. These findings suggest that everyday environments can communicate who belongs in a field. They do not imply that a teacher’s gender alone determines outcomes; teaching quality, course assignment, student characteristics, and institutional context must also be considered.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the messages about belonging that students receive from faculty composition, leadership visibility, and classroom participation."
Key point:
- Repeated exposure to counter-stereotypic leaders can shape expectations.
- Representation is one part of an environment that also includes teaching, opportunity, and institutional culture.
Source links:
- Dasgupta and Asgari (2004): Exposure to Counterstereotypic Women Leaders
- Carrell, Page and West (2009): Sex and Science
40. Q: What did the chapter’s examples of L3, WISE, and WSAS have in common?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes three programmes that connect girls with women working in science, technology, engineering, mathematics, and research. L3 brought women scientists to an elementary school for informal conversations. WISE connected high-school students with advanced instruction, university laboratories, research experiences, and career models. WSAS arranged visits by women scientists to local high schools so students could hear about their education, work, and career paths. The shared design feature is repeated, concrete exposure to people who make a field visible and approachable. These examples illustrate possible programme models; they are not presented as randomized trials proving a particular effect size.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the three programmes by age group, frequency, hands-on experience, and type of role-model contact."
Key point:
- Role-model programmes can combine visibility, mentoring, and practical exposure.
- Illustrative programmes should not be confused with controlled evidence of impact.
Source links:
- Dasgupta and Asgari (2004): Exposure to Counterstereotypic Women Leaders
- Carrell, Page and West (2009): Sex and Science
41. Q: How should an organization design a role-model or mentoring programme responsibly?
A: Show answer
Begin with the capability or opportunity gap the programme is intended to address. Recruit role models whose expertise and experience are relevant, and provide structured opportunities for learners to ask questions, observe work, practise skills, and receive feedback. Avoid relying on one symbolic speaker or expecting underrepresented staff to perform unpaid representation work without support. Measure participation, access to opportunities, belonging, retention, skill development, and longer-term outcomes rather than counting events alone. Mentoring should connect to fair recruitment, transparent evaluation, safe reporting channels, and advancement pathways. A programme is credible when it changes access and experience, not merely the organization’s public image.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a programme logic model linking role-model contact to skill development, opportunity, and measurable outcomes."
Key point:
- Role-model exposure is more useful when connected to real learning and opportunity.
- Programme evaluation should measure outcomes, not only activity counts.
Source links:
- Dasgupta and Asgari (2004): Exposure to Counterstereotypic Women Leaders
- Carrell, Page and West (2009): Sex and Science
42. Q: What is the recursive cycle between test performance, confidence, and future learning?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains how one pressured test can influence later choices. A poor result may lower confidence and encourage a student to avoid the subject; reduced participation then means less practice and knowledge, which can lead to another poor result. A more positive result may increase confidence, encourage further study, and create opportunities for advanced coursework. This is a recursive process because each outcome can change the conditions for the next outcome. The cycle is not automatic or irreversible. Feedback, teaching quality, resources, encouragement, and later experiences can interrupt a negative trajectory. A single test should therefore not become a permanent label of a student’s potential.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a feedback loop showing how performance affects confidence, course choices, practice, and subsequent performance."
Key point:
- Performance can influence later opportunities and self-beliefs.
- Early setbacks need not determine a person’s long-term pathway.
Source links:
- Cohen et al. (2009): Recursive Processes in Self-Affirmation
43. Q: How can students practise under pressure without turning every study session into a stressful event?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends rehearsing the conditions that matter for the actual assessment: time limits, independent retrieval, realistic question formats, and reduced reliance on study aids when those aids will not be available during the test. Practice should be progressive. Begin with manageable tasks, then introduce timing and exam-like conditions as competence grows. Review errors calmly so that practice builds information about gaps rather than merely repeating pressure. The goal is familiarity with the task demands, not chronic stress exposure. Students should also protect sleep, recovery, and study breaks; practising while exhausted may rehearse poor performance rather than build skill.
Study instruction:
- "Create a staged practice plan that moves from untimed learning to realistic timed retrieval and feedback."
Key point:
- Practising relevant test conditions can reduce unfamiliarity with the assessment.
- Pressure practice should be progressive and paired with feedback and recovery.
Source links:
- Klein and Boals (2001): Expressive Writing and Working Memory
44. Q: Why is retrieval practice often more useful than only rereading notes?
A: Show answer
Retrieval practice requires a learner to bring information to mind without simply looking at the answer. The chapter recommends testing oneself because the assessment itself requires retrieval under conditions that may limit access to notes. Short quizzes, blank-page recall, explaining a concept from memory, and solving unseen problems reveal what can actually be retrieved. Rereading can create familiarity without proving that the information is accessible when needed. Retrieval practice should be followed by feedback so errors are corrected rather than rehearsed. It should also be distributed over time and matched to the kind of performance required.
Study instruction:
- "Replace one rereading session with a closed-book recall exercise and compare the gaps revealed by each method."
Key point:
- Practising retrieval prepares the learner for the act required during an assessment.
- Use feedback to correct errors and strengthen later recall.
Source links:
- Klein and Boals (2001): Expressive Writing and Working Memory
45. Q: What does it mean to outsource cognitive load during a difficult problem?
A: Show answer
Outsourcing cognitive load means moving information that does not need to remain in working memory onto paper or another permitted external aid. A student can write down known values, intermediate calculations, assumptions, sub-steps, and a short checklist of conditions. This reduces the risk of forgetting a value or confusing the order of operations while attention is directed to the next step. External notes are especially useful for multi-stage calculations and complex instructions. They must be organized and legible; a disordered page can create additional cognitive load. The method should comply with assessment rules and should support reasoning rather than replace understanding.
Study instruction:
- "Solve a multi-step problem using a written sequence and mark each completed step before moving to the next."
Key point:
- External notes reduce the amount of information that must be held mentally.
- A clear written structure supports reasoning but does not replace understanding.
Source links:
- Liston, McEwen and Casey (2009): Psychosocial Stress and Prefrontal Processing
46. Q: How can organizing knowledge reduce pressure on working memory?
A: Show answer
Organizing knowledge means arranging information into meaningful categories, relationships, patterns, or procedures before it is needed. A student who understands how formulas relate to problem types can recognize a structure rather than treating every question as a new collection of facts. A speaker who groups key points into a logical outline can retrieve the argument more easily than someone trying to remember a long unstructured script. The chapter refers to expert waiters as an example of the value of meaningful organization: skilled performers use structures that help them remember complex orders. The practical lesson is to build schemas, summaries, concept maps, and checklists that reduce unnecessary search during performance.
Study instruction:
- "Convert one topic into a concept map or decision tree and use it to answer a new question without looking at the original notes."
Key point:
- Meaningful organization makes information easier to retrieve and apply.
- Structure reduces mental search and supports flexible use of knowledge.
Source links:
- Liston, McEwen and Casey (2009): Psychosocial Stress and Prefrontal Processing
47. Q: What is the “pause your choke” or incubation strategy?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends briefly stepping away from a difficult problem when repeated effort is producing no progress, particularly when the task heavily depends on working memory. A short break can interrupt fixation on irrelevant details and allow the person to approach the problem from a different perspective when returning. This is often called incubation. It should be a deliberate pause, not indefinite avoidance: record what has been tried, identify the unresolved point, step away briefly, and return with a fresh attempt. The strategy is most useful when continued effort has become repetitive or counterproductive. It is not a substitute for learning foundational material or managing limited examination time.
Study instruction:
- "Use a planned short break after repeated unproductive attempts and record whether the next attempt uses a different approach."
Key point:
- A brief pause can interrupt fixation and create space for a new perspective.
- Incubation should be bounded and followed by a deliberate return to the problem.
Source links:
- Liston, McEwen and Casey (2009): Psychosocial Stress and Prefrontal Processing
48. Q: How can a person reinterpret physical signs of arousal before a test or presentation?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests that signs such as a racing heart or sweaty palms can be interpreted as activation rather than automatic evidence of failure. The same bodily sensations can accompany excitement, anticipation, or fear. Reframing them as “my body is preparing for a demanding task” may reduce the extra worry created by treating arousal as proof that something is wrong. This is a cognitive reappraisal strategy, not a claim that all anxiety is harmless or that physical symptoms should be ignored. If symptoms are severe, new, or medically concerning, they should be assessed appropriately. For ordinary performance nerves, the person can acknowledge the sensations and focus on the next controllable action.
Study instruction:
- "Write two interpretations of a common stress reaction and identify which one supports a constructive next step without denying the feeling."
Key point:
- The meaning assigned to arousal can influence the secondary worry around it.
- Reappraisal is not a substitute for medical or psychological care when symptoms require it.
Source links:
- Lieberman et al. (2007): Putting Feelings Into Words
- Liston, McEwen and Casey (2009): Psychosocial Stress and Prefrontal Processing
49. Q: How can students use the chapter’s “reaffirm your self-worth” strategy before a major assessment?
A: Show answer
Before a test or presentation, spend a short period writing about interests, relationships, values, activities, or accomplishments that matter independently of the upcoming result. The aim is to remember that the assessment is one event within a larger life, not a complete judgment of personal value. Use specific examples rather than generic affirmations. The exercise may be especially relevant when the person feels that one performance will define their identity. It should not be used to dismiss real preparation needs or structural barriers. If it helps, the student can then move directly into a practical plan for the first task step.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a two-minute values reflection and then identify the first concrete action required by the assessment."
Key point:
- A broader sense of self-worth can reduce the pressure attached to one result.
- Reflection should support preparation, not replace it.
Source links:
- Cohen et al. (2006): Reducing the Racial Achievement Gap
- Cohen et al. (2009): Recursive Processes in Self-Affirmation
50. Q: What does “map out your complexities” mean as a practical preparation exercise?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests drawing a simple diagram of the different roles, interests, relationships, skills, and experiences that make a person who they are. For example, a student might include family responsibilities, a part-time job, music, sport, volunteering, friendships, and academic interests. The exercise makes visible that a single score cannot represent the whole person. It can also remind the student of evidence of persistence, learning, and capability from other settings. The map need not be shared with anyone. Its purpose is personal perspective, not a formal personality assessment or a ranking of identities.
Study instruction:
- "Create a private identity map and circle three strengths or commitments that remain true regardless of the test outcome."
Key point:
- A multifaceted identity can put one assessment into a wider perspective.
- The exercise is reflective and personal, not a measure of intelligence or worth.
Source links:
- Shih, Pittinsky and Ambady (1999): Identity Salience and Quantitative Performance
51. Q: How can educators reduce unnecessary identity threat in the way they introduce an assessment?
A: Show answer
Educators can explain the purpose of the assessment, what it measures, how results will be used, and what they do not measure. Avoid framing a task as proof that one group is naturally better or worse. Collect demographic information only when needed and consider whether it can be requested after the assessment. Use clear instructions, consistent administration, accessible materials, and transparent scoring. Feedback should focus on specific work and next steps rather than broad labels such as “not a maths person.” These practices cannot eliminate every social threat, but they reduce avoidable cues that can make the assessment feel like a judgment of group identity or personal worth.
Study instruction:
- "Review an assessment script and remove language that implies the test is a verdict on identity or fixed ability."
Key point:
- Assessment framing can affect how learners interpret the task.
- Clear, respectful instructions and transparent use of results reduce avoidable threat cues.
Source links:
- Danaher and Crandall (2008): Stereotype Threat in Applied Settings
- Johns, Schmader and Martens (2005): Teaching Stereotype Threat
- Stoevenbelt et al. (2026): Registered Replication Report
52. Q: How should a teacher respond when a student underperforms under pressure?
A: Show answer
Start by examining the evidence without assuming that the score reflects fixed ability or that stereotype threat must be the cause. Compare performance across practice, classwork, and assessment; ask about preparation, task understanding, sleep, time management, and perceived pressure; and look for patterns in errors. Offer specific feedback and a chance to demonstrate learning again where policy allows. If the student reports recurring anxiety or exclusion, listen and connect them with appropriate support. Avoid singling out a student as representative of a group. The goal is to identify what can be improved in teaching, assessment, preparation, and environment.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a response protocol that separates evidence review, student discussion, instructional support, and referral where needed."
Key point:
- A low score is evidence about one performance, not a complete measure of potential.
- Respond with diagnosis and support rather than assumptions about the student or group.
Source links:
- Liston, McEwen and Casey (2009): Psychosocial Stress and Prefrontal Processing
- Stoevenbelt et al. (2026): Registered Replication Report
53. Q: How can institutions use the chapter’s ideas without placing the burden entirely on students?
A: Show answer
Individual strategies such as writing, mindfulness, self-affirmation, and role-model exposure can be useful options, but institutions control many conditions that create or reduce stress. They can provide fair assessment procedures, adequate preparation, accessible facilities, transparent evaluation, representative teaching and leadership, respectful classroom norms, timely feedback, and safe ways to report discrimination. They can also monitor whether particular groups experience avoidable barriers. Telling students to become more resilient while leaving hostile or inequitable conditions unchanged would misapply the chapter. The strongest approach combines individual skills with changes to the environment that repeatedly generates threat.
Study instruction:
- "Audit one learning environment for personal supports and institutional conditions that contribute to avoidable pressure."
Key point:
- Personal coping tools cannot substitute for fair and inclusive institutional practices.
- Sustainable improvement requires both individual support and environmental change.
Source links:
- Dasgupta and Asgari (2004): Exposure to Counterstereotypic Women Leaders
- Carrell, Page and West (2009): Sex and Science
- Stoevenbelt et al. (2026): Registered Replication Report
54. Q: When should persistent stress be treated as more than ordinary performance anxiety?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses stress in relation to cognition, but persistent or severe distress may require support beyond study techniques. Warning signs can include ongoing sleep disruption, panic, intrusive thoughts that interfere with daily life, avoidance of important activities, sustained low mood, or inability to recover after the stressor has passed. A student or employee should be encouraged to contact an appropriate health professional, counselling service, or trusted support channel when symptoms are persistent or impairing. A short writing exercise or meditation practice should not be presented as treatment for trauma, an anxiety disorder, or another clinical condition. Organizational responses should also address the source of chronic stress where possible.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the difference between ordinary test nerves and persistent impairment, and list appropriate support routes in your setting."
Key point:
- Self-help techniques have limits when distress is persistent or clinically significant.
- Use qualified support for severe or ongoing symptoms and address the source of chronic stress.
Source links:
- Liston, McEwen and Casey (2009): Psychosocial Stress and Prefrontal Processing
- Klein and Boals (2001): Expressive Writing and Working Memory
55. Q: How can a student combine the chapter’s strategies into a practical pre-test routine?
A: Show answer
A useful routine begins well before test day with organized knowledge, retrieval practice, and realistic timed exercises. Shortly before the assessment, the student can review the task format, write briefly about current worries if that is helpful, reaffirm values or broader identity, and use a short attention practice. During the test, they can read instructions carefully, write intermediate steps, label a distracting worry briefly, and return attention to the next actionable item. If stuck, they can mark the problem and move on where the rules allow, then return later. Afterward, review errors and identify whether they arose from knowledge gaps, task strategy, time pressure, or distraction.
Study instruction:
- "Create a routine for the day before, the first five minutes, the test itself, and the post-test review."
Key point:
- Combine preparation, attention management, and external structure rather than relying on one technique.
- Select strategies that fit the person, assessment rules, and task demands.
Source links:
- Klein and Boals (2001): Expressive Writing and Working Memory
- Slagter et al. (2007): Mental Training and Limited Brain Resources
- Zainal and Newman (2024): Meta-Analysis of 111 Mindfulness RCTs
56. Q: How can organizations prepare employees for high-stakes presentations or decisions using the chapter’s principles?
A: Show answer
Organizations can reduce avoidable cognitive load by clarifying the purpose, decision criteria, time available, roles, and evidence expected before the meeting. Participants should receive materials early, use a structured agenda, and be allowed to record intermediate reasoning rather than relying on memory. Leaders can set norms that questions and dissent are legitimate, avoid framing one presentation as a test of personal worth, and build realistic rehearsal opportunities. A short pause before a complex decision can help participants regain perspective. These measures support performance without promising that anxiety will disappear. They also improve the quality of the decision by making the task more transparent and the evidence easier to evaluate.
Study instruction:
- "Design a preparation checklist for a high-stakes meeting covering clarity, rehearsal, materials, roles, and psychological safety."
Key point:
- Clear structure reduces avoidable cognitive demands in high-pressure work.
- A supportive process improves both performance conditions and decision quality.
Source links:
- Liston, McEwen and Casey (2009): Psychosocial Stress and Prefrontal Processing
- Lieberman et al. (2007): Putting Feelings Into Words
57. Q: What are the main risks of relying too heavily on quick “choking cure” techniques?
A: Show answer
Quick techniques can be overpromised, applied without regard to the person, or used to avoid addressing the real cause of poor performance. A student may be told to meditate when the main problem is inadequate instruction; an employee may be told to reframe anxiety while facing discriminatory treatment; a school may adopt a one-time writing exercise instead of improving access and teaching. Some techniques have mixed evidence, and a strategy that helps one person may distract or distress another. There is also a risk of treating a score as a complete measure of ability or interpreting a small study as a universal rule. The chapter is best used as a menu of testable supports within a broader system of preparation and fairness.
Study instruction:
- "For each proposed technique, identify its target problem, evidence, possible downside, and the broader issue it cannot solve."
Key point:
- No single technique is a universal cure for performance under pressure.
- Do not use individual coping advice to conceal poor systems or structural barriers.
Source links:
- Hanselman et al. (2017): Large-Scale Self-Affirmation Replication
- Baker, Skinner and Redding (2020): Self-Affirmation in a Community College
- Stoevenbelt et al. (2026): Registered Replication Report
- Im et al. (2021): Mindfulness and Cognitive Function Meta-Analysis
58. Q: How should the chapter’s research findings be translated into workplace or educational policy?
A: Show answer
Translate findings into testable hypotheses rather than blanket rules. For example, an organization might hypothesize that collecting demographic data after an assessment reduces unnecessary identity salience, or that a brief values-reflection exercise improves engagement for a particular learner group. Define the target population, intervention, comparison, outcome, time frame, and potential harms before implementation. Use appropriate evaluation methods and report null as well as positive results. Consider whether an intervention works only under particular conditions and whether a broader policy change is needed. Scale only when results are credible, implementation is feasible, and the practice respects privacy, dignity, and fairness.
Study instruction:
- "Turn one chapter recommendation into a pilot with a comparison condition, measurable outcomes, and a decision rule for continuation."
Key point:
- Evidence should inform policy through evaluation, not be copied as a universal prescription.
- Report null findings and unintended effects as part of responsible implementation.
Source links:
- Liu et al. (2021): Meta-Analysis of Stereotype-Threat Interventions
- Hanselman et al. (2017): Large-Scale Self-Affirmation Replication
- Baker, Skinner and Redding (2020): Self-Affirmation in a Community College
- Stoevenbelt et al. (2026): Registered Replication Report
59. Q: What is the difference between improving performance and improving underlying competence?
A: Show answer
Improving performance means helping a person demonstrate what they currently know or can do under the conditions of an assessment. Improving competence means developing the knowledge, skill, judgment, and practice that make stronger performance possible over time. The chapter’s strategies mainly aim to reduce interference so existing ability can be expressed more accurately. They do not replace instruction, practice, feedback, or skill development. A student who writes about worries may approach a test with fewer distractions, but still needs to learn the mathematics. Likewise, a professional who uses mindfulness before a presentation still needs sound analysis and preparation. Both aims matter, but they require different interventions and measures.
Study instruction:
- "Classify each proposed intervention as primarily reducing performance interference or developing underlying competence."
Key point:
- Coping strategies can help express ability but do not create subject knowledge by themselves.
- Combine performance support with sustained learning and deliberate practice.
Source links:
- Klein and Boals (2001): Expressive Writing and Working Memory
- Slagter et al. (2007): Mental Training and Limited Brain Resources
60. Q: How can an assessment distinguish knowledge gaps from pressure-related execution errors?
A: Show answer
Use multiple forms of evidence. Compare low-stakes practice, untimed problem-solving, timed simulations, oral explanations, and the final assessment. Examine the pattern of errors: conceptual misunderstanding may persist across settings, while time pressure or distraction may produce omissions, skipped steps, or inconsistent performance. Ask learners to explain their reasoning and note where they lost track. Repeat assessments where appropriate and permitted, using comparable tasks rather than assuming one result is definitive. The aim is to determine whether the main need is additional instruction, strategy training, stress support, assessment redesign, or a combination.
Study instruction:
- "Build an evidence matrix comparing practice, timed work, explanation, and assessment results to identify likely causes of underperformance."
Key point:
- Different error patterns point to different support needs.
- Use more than one assessment condition before drawing conclusions about ability.
Source links:
- Liston, McEwen and Casey (2009): Psychosocial Stress and Prefrontal Processing
61. Q: What does the chapter teach about the relationship between confidence and performance?
A: Show answer
Confidence can influence whether a person approaches a task, persists after difficulty, and interprets setbacks as temporary or defining. The chapter’s examples suggest that self-worth, role models, and a broader identity can reduce the weight of negative expectations. However, confidence should be grounded in preparation and evidence rather than inflated reassurance. A student who believes they can improve is more likely to practise and seek feedback, but confidence alone does not guarantee mastery. Similarly, one poor result should not be interpreted as proof of inability. Constructive confidence combines realistic self-assessment, knowledge of next steps, and a willingness to learn from performance.
Study instruction:
- "Write a realistic confidence statement that acknowledges a current gap and identifies a concrete action to address it."
Key point:
- Confidence is most useful when linked to evidence, effort, and a learning plan.
- A setback is information for improvement, not a permanent identity label.
Source links:
- Cohen et al. (2009): Recursive Processes in Self-Affirmation
- Dasgupta and Asgari (2004): Exposure to Counterstereotypic Women Leaders
62. Q: What are the most important lessons to retain from Chapter 6?
A: Show answer
Chapter 6 explains that pressure can interfere with the coordinated attention and working-memory processes needed to show what a person knows. It presents several ways to reduce that interference: affirm meaningful values, write about worries, name emotions, train attention, broaden the self-concept, encounter credible role models, reduce unnecessary stereotype cues, practise under realistic conditions, externalize intermediate steps, organize knowledge, reinterpret ordinary arousal, and take a short incubation break when stuck. The research examples are informative but vary in strength and generalizability. Later replications show that some interventions do not work reliably in every setting. The enduring lesson is to combine evidence-informed personal strategies with fair environments, sustained preparation, and careful evaluation.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the mechanisms, strategies, evidence limitations, and institutional responsibilities without treating any one method as a guaranteed cure."
Key point:
- Performance under pressure can improve when avoidable cognitive and social burdens are reduced.
- Use strategies critically, support the whole person, and improve the environment as well as individual preparation.
Source links:
- Liston, McEwen and Casey (2009): Psychosocial Stress and Prefrontal Processing
- Klein and Boals (2001): Expressive Writing and Working Memory
- Lieberman et al. (2007): Putting Feelings Into Words
- Zainal and Newman (2024): Meta-Analysis of 111 Mindfulness RCTs
- Stoevenbelt et al. (2026): Registered Replication Report
Rows: 62
Table 7
Choking Under Pressure: Skill Automaticity, Attention, and the Yips
1. Q: What does Chapter 7 mean by choking under pressure?
A: Show answer
Choking is a marked form of suboptimal performance in which a person performs below a level that the person has previously demonstrated and is capable of achieving, in a situation perceived as highly stressful. It is not simply any mistake, loss, or disappointing result. The chapter links the performance decline to pressure-related processes such as worry, heightened self-consciousness, excessive conscious control, or impaired movement execution. A skilled golfer missing a routine putt after becoming preoccupied with the consequences may be choking; a novice missing the same putt may simply lack the skill. The defining comparison is with the performer's established capability under less pressurized conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Define choking using both the performance gap and the perceived-pressure condition, then apply the definition to a familiar skill."
Key point:
- Choking is suboptimal performance relative to demonstrated capability.
- Pressure-related processes help explain the decline.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
2. Q: How can choking be distinguished from ordinary poor performance or an opponent's excellent performance?
A: Show answer
A poor result alone does not establish choking. The evaluator must ask whether the performer possessed the relevant skill, normally performed better, perceived the situation as pressurized, and showed a performance decline associated with that pressure. In direct competition, an opponent's improvement can make one athlete look worse even when the athlete performs normally. The chapter therefore distinguishes a genuine breakdown from a comeback or superior execution by another competitor. Useful evidence includes the performer's own prior performance, error patterns, pressure conditions, and whether the failure is unusual for that person. This avoids labeling every defeat as a psychological failure.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a pressured performance with the person's prior baseline and examine whether the opponent's play explains the outcome."
Key point:
- A loss is not automatically a choke.
- The comparison must be against the performer's own demonstrated ability.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
3. Q: Why can an important opportunity make a skilled performer more vulnerable to choking?
A: Show answer
High-stakes opportunities increase the perceived cost of failure. A performer may focus on losing a title, disappointing teammates, damaging a reputation, or wasting years of preparation. These consequences can make attention shift from the task to the self and the possible outcome. The chapter's examples include athletes who were close to winning and then made unusual errors or poor decisions. The critical mechanism is not that success itself causes failure; rather, the importance attached to success can trigger worry and attempts to control performance in ways that interfere with established skills. Expectations, audience attention, and personal identity can amplify that process.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the consequences a performer is imagining and distinguish task cues from outcome-focused thoughts."
Key point:
- High stakes can intensify self-monitoring and worry.
- The goal is to keep attention useful to the task rather than the feared result.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
4. Q: What do the chapter's sporting examples demonstrate about the forms choking can take?
A: Show answer
The examples span team competition, individual sports, precision actions, and decisions. The Yankees' collapse illustrates a team losing a series after being close to victory; Greg Norman and Kenny Perry illustrate late tournament declines; Jean Van de Velde illustrates a sequence of poor decisions under pressure; and missed free throws, penalty kicks, and a costly own-goal illustrate execution failures. Lindsey Jacobellis's final-jump decision shows that choking can occur before a movement is executed. These cases are illustrations, not proof that every high-profile failure has the same cause. They show why the specific task and failure mechanism must be examined.
Study instruction:
- "Classify each example as an execution failure, a decision failure, or a team-level performance decline."
Key point:
- Choking can affect individuals, teams, decisions, and motor execution.
- Different failure mechanisms require different analysis.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
5. Q: How does worry interfere with performance on tasks that depend heavily on working memory?
A: Show answer
Worry occupies attention and working-memory capacity that would otherwise be available for holding instructions, comparing alternatives, updating information, and reasoning. A student solving a demanding problem or a spokesperson answering difficult questions may use working memory to maintain several pieces of information at once. If the person also rehearses feared outcomes, imagines embarrassment, or tries to suppress intrusive thoughts, fewer cognitive resources remain for the task. The chapter treats worry as a direct threat to cognitively demanding performance. The effect is especially relevant when the task itself requires deliberate reasoning rather than a highly automated motor routine.
Study instruction:
- "List the information a demanding task requires you to hold in mind and identify which worries compete for that capacity."
Key point:
- Worry can consume working-memory resources.
- Cognitive tasks are vulnerable when little capacity remains for reasoning.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
6. Q: Why is worry not necessarily the direct cause of choking in a highly practised motor skill?
A: Show answer
A well-learned motor skill may rely less on deliberate working-memory operations than a complex reasoning task. Its execution is supported by procedural learning and distributed motor networks. Worry can still be present, but the chapter argues that the more direct route to failure is often what worry prompts the performer to do: consciously inspect and control movements that normally run fluidly. Thus, the same emotional state can impair different tasks through different mechanisms. In a reasoning task, worry may occupy cognitive capacity; in an automated swing or putt, worry may trigger excessive self-monitoring and disrupt movement.
Study instruction:
- "For a cognitive task and an automated motor task, explain the different routes from worry to performance decline."
Key point:
- Worry does not affect every task through one mechanism.
- Identify whether the main risk is cognitive distraction or over-control of movement.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
7. Q: What is procedural knowledge, and how does it support skilled movement?
A: Show answer
Procedural knowledge is learned know-how for carrying out an action, rather than a verbal description of every step. Repeated practice builds coordinated patterns that can be initiated and adjusted without consciously calculating each joint movement. The chapter associates skilled motor performance with networks that include the basal ganglia and motor systems, reducing reliance on the prefrontal working-memory resources needed for step-by-step control. A practiced golfer does not normally narrate the exact wrist angle, elbow position, and timing of every part of a swing. The skill is expressed through an integrated action pattern. Conscious instructions remain useful during learning or technique change, but can interfere when inserted unnecessarily during fluent execution.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast a verbal rule for performing a movement with the practiced ability to execute it smoothly."
Key point:
- Procedural learning supports efficient skilled action.
- A practiced movement is not normally controlled as a list of conscious steps.
Source links:
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
8. Q: What is self-focused attention during skilled performance?
A: Show answer
Self-focused attention occurs when a performer turns attention inward toward personal movements, body positions, or the process of executing a familiar skill. Under pressure, the performer may monitor the wrist, grip, stance, breathing, release point, or finger movement instead of attending to the task-relevant outcome. The chapter describes this as a shift from allowing a practiced action to run to consciously supervising its components. Self-awareness is not always harmful: it can be valuable during instruction, correction, or unfamiliar actions. It becomes risky when a performer applies detailed conscious control to a stable skill whose coordination has already been learned.
Study instruction:
- "Record where your attention goes during a familiar skill and determine whether each cue helps execution or interrupts it."
Key point:
- Self-focus can disrupt well-learned movement.
- The usefulness of attention depends on the stage and nature of the skill.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
9. Q: What is paralysis by analysis, and why can it cause a skilled performer to choke?
A: Show answer
Paralysis by analysis is a breakdown that can occur when a performer consciously inspects and controls details of a movement that normally operate automatically. A practiced basketball player may start monitoring wrist angle and release point during a decisive free throw; a golfer may think through each element of a routine putt. This extra supervision can interrupt timing and coordination. The problem is not analysis in every context, but analysis at the wrong moment and level of detail. During learning, conscious attention may improve technique. During fluent execution, excessive step-by-step monitoring can make the action slower, less coordinated, or more error-prone.
Study instruction:
- "Take a practiced movement and identify which details should be addressed in training but not consciously monitored during normal execution."
Key point:
- Paralysis by analysis is over-control of an established skill.
- Conscious detail can help learning yet harm fluent execution.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
10. Q: How can negative self-talk and mental images contribute to choking?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that performers who imagine themselves failing may become more likely to make the feared error. A golfer may rehearse a missed putt, a dart player may picture the dart missing the target, or a basketball player may repeatedly think about missing a decisive free throw. These thoughts increase worry and draw attention toward the self and the consequences of failure. The performer may then alter a routine or try to control movements that previously worked. The chapter does not imply that every negative thought mechanically causes a mistake; rather, recurring failure imagery is a risk factor because it changes attention, confidence, and preparation.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the failure image that appears before a high-pressure attempt and replace it with a concrete task-relevant cue."
Key point:
- Failure-focused imagery can amplify worry.
- Attention is more useful when directed toward an actionable task cue.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
11. Q: What are mirror or action-observation networks, and why are they relevant to expertise?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes brain networks involved both in observing actions and in producing similar actions. When highly skilled dancers watched movements from their own repertoire, action-related regions such as premotor and parietal areas were engaged more than when they watched unfamiliar movements. The idea is that skilled observers can internally simulate actions they know how to perform. This may help them recognize movement patterns and anticipate what will happen next. The term mirror network is a broad description of overlapping perception-action processes, not a claim that one isolated neuron explains expertise. The chapter uses the evidence to explain how practiced performers can read situations quickly.
Study instruction:
- "Compare how an expert and a novice might interpret a familiar action sequence and explain the role of learned motor experience."
Key point:
- Expertise can link action observation with internal motor representations.
- A skilled performer may anticipate familiar actions before they unfold.
Source links:
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
12. Q: How does expertise help athletes anticipate actions before they happen?
A: Show answer
Repeated experience gives performers a rich store of patterns linking visible cues to likely outcomes. A snowboarder can prepare for landing before leaving a jump, a goalkeeper may begin moving before the ball is struck, and a skier may plan several gates ahead. The performer is not predicting with certainty; the person is using learned regularities to prepare for probable next events. This anticipatory capacity reduces the need to wait for complete feedback after every action. Under pressure, however, excessive conscious monitoring can interfere with the smooth use of those learned predictions. Expertise therefore depends not only on knowing what to do but also on allowing well-developed anticipation to guide timely action.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a fast-paced activity and identify the cues an expert can use to anticipate the next event."
Key point:
- Practice builds predictive knowledge of familiar situations.
- Anticipation allows action to begin before full feedback is available.
Source links:
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
13. Q: Why can a supportive audience sometimes make a skilled performer do worse?
A: Show answer
Supportive observers can increase self-awareness because the performer becomes conscious of being watched and of the audience's expectations. That heightened awareness may prompt the person to monitor the execution of a familiar skill. The audience may be kind and the performer may feel emotionally supported, yet performance can still deteriorate if attention shifts inward. The chapter's example of Mia's mother shows how a routine putt becomes socially consequential when other players are watching. The effect is not universal: some performers feed on an audience. The lesson is that emotional comfort and motor execution are separate dimensions, and a supportive setting can still create evaluative pressure.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish whether an audience makes you feel supported, evaluated, self-conscious, or some combination of these."
Key point:
- Supportive intent does not guarantee improved execution.
- Being observed can heighten self-focus even when observers are friendly.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
14. Q: How can an audience feel less stressful while still impairing performance?
A: Show answer
A performer may report feeling less anxious in front of a supportive observer than in front of a neutral or indifferent observer, yet still perform worse. The chapter's discussion of the Sky Jinks experiment illustrates this distinction: supportive presence appeared to increase attention to the self and performance, even though participants experienced the situation as less stressful. Subjective stress ratings therefore do not always reveal the attentional process that affects execution. Coaches should not assume that a friendly atmosphere automatically protects an automated skill from over-monitoring. They should examine what the performer attends to and how the action changes.
Study instruction:
- "Compare reported anxiety with actual accuracy or movement quality instead of treating them as identical measures."
Key point:
- Feeling supported and executing well are not the same outcome.
- Assess both emotional experience and task performance.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
15. Q: What did the chapter's discussion of championship home teams suggest about audience pressure?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts research in which home teams with a chance to clinch a championship sometimes performed worse than expected, including more unforced errors. One proposed explanation is that a supportive home audience can increase self-consciousness and encourage players to control movements that normally run automatically. The result challenges the simplistic assumption that home support always improves performance. It should not be generalized to every sport, team, or crowd: the chapter also notes debate about how to interpret championship-pressure data. The useful conclusion is that audience effects depend on the task, context, and performer's response.
Study instruction:
- "When reviewing a home-field advantage claim, separate crowd support, expectation pressure, opponent performance, and unforced errors."
Key point:
- A home crowd can create both support and evaluative pressure.
- Context matters; audience effects are not universal.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
16. Q: What does the Sky Jinks experiment add to the explanation of supportive-audience effects?
A: Show answer
In the experiment described in the chapter, participants first practiced a joystick-controlled video game until they became skilled. They then played for an incentive while an observer behaved either supportively or neutrally. Participants performed worse with the supportive observer, and the chapter links this result to greater self-focus. The design is useful because the task was learned before pressure was introduced, allowing researchers to examine how evaluation affects a practiced coordination skill. It does not prove that all supportive audiences harm performance, but it demonstrates a plausible mechanism by which attention to being watched can disrupt an established action.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why participants were trained before the observer condition and what comparison the experiment made possible."
Key point:
- The experiment tested pressure after skill acquisition.
- Supportive observation can increase self-focus and impair a practiced task.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
17. Q: What are degrees of freedom in motor control?
A: Show answer
Degrees of freedom are the multiple movement possibilities available across joints and body segments when coordinating an action. A throw, kick, piano passage, or golf swing can be produced through many combinations of wrist, elbow, shoulder, hip, knee, and ankle movement. A novice initially faces a coordination problem because many possibilities must be organized into a stable and effective pattern. Early learning may therefore involve restricting movement options by stiffening joints or linking them together. With practice, the performer learns to coordinate more flexible and independent movements. The concept explains why skill is not simply strength or speed; it also requires managing the body's many possible movement combinations.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the joints involved in a familiar action and describe how their movements must be coordinated."
Key point:
- Motor skills involve coordinating multiple degrees of freedom.
- Effective practice develops flexible, task-appropriate coordination.
Source links:
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
18. Q: Why might pressure cause a skilled performer to freeze degrees of freedom?
A: Show answer
Under pressure, a performer may try to regain control by making movement components more rigid or by coupling joints that normally move independently. The chapter describes this as a return toward a beginner-like strategy. A weightlifter may tighten coordination between neck and hip movements, a climber may move more rigidly when high above the ground, and a musician may lose independent finger movement. The extra rigidity can reduce the flexibility and timing required for skilled execution. This is not a deliberate strategy chosen after careful analysis; it can emerge when anxiety and conscious control disrupt the practiced coordination pattern.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a movement under relaxed and pressured conditions and note whether joints become more rigid or move together."
Key point:
- Pressure can reduce movement flexibility.
- Freezing movement components can disrupt the coordination built through practice.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
19. Q: How can freezing and excessive joint coupling affect musicians as well as athletes?
A: Show answer
Many musical performances require precise, independent timing of fingers, hands, wrists, and arms. A pianist performing a complex passage may need each hand to execute a different pattern while maintaining coordinated timing. If anxiety causes the musician to consciously control every finger or stiffen the hands, movements can become coupled and less fluent. The same principle appears in sports when a practiced action depends on independent joint coordination. The chapter therefore treats choking as a general problem of skilled sensorimotor performance, not an issue limited to athletes. The specific movement demands differ, but excessive control can interfere with learned fluency.
Study instruction:
- "Select a musical or athletic skill and identify which movements must remain independent for accurate execution."
Key point:
- The mechanism applies across different skilled activities.
- Fluency depends on coordinated, appropriately independent movement.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
20. Q: When is conscious attention to technique beneficial rather than harmful?
A: Show answer
Conscious attention is useful when a person is learning a new skill, correcting a specific technical error, or deliberately changing a movement pattern. A beginner may need to focus on foot contact, body position, or sequence because the action has not yet become stable. Attention is also valuable when a performer faces a novel decision, an unfamiliar environment, or a genuine safety concern. The risk arises when a skilled performer applies detailed step-by-step monitoring to an action that is already well learned and normally automatic. Coaches should therefore distinguish practice designed to change technique from competition designed to execute the current technique.
Study instruction:
- "Classify each training activity as skill acquisition, technique correction, or performance execution and choose the appropriate attentional focus."
Key point:
- Attention is not inherently harmful.
- Its value depends on whether the performer is learning, adapting, or executing.
Source links:
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
21. Q: Why can deliberate decision-making help in a novel situation but hinder an expert in a familiar one?
A: Show answer
A novel situation may contain unfamiliar risks, incomplete information, or consequences that require deliberate evaluation. In that setting, working memory can help the performer compare options and consider outcomes. In a familiar situation, extensive deliberation may add little because experience has already built fast pattern recognition and action selection. The chapter's handball example shows skilled players often selected the best move quickly before being asked to list further options. The lesson is not to reject reasoning, but to match the decision process to the situation: deliberate when novelty demands analysis, and avoid needless re-analysis of familiar patterns.
Study instruction:
- "For one familiar and one novel scenario, explain why the appropriate balance between intuition and deliberate reasoning differs."
Key point:
- Expert intuition can be valuable in familiar situations.
- Novel or high-consequence situations may require explicit evaluation.
Source links:
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
22. Q: What did the handball decision study reveal about the first option selected by skilled players?
A: Show answer
In the study described, experienced handball players watched game sequences and selected the best next action when the video paused. They then considered other possible actions. Coaches judged the first option to be the best more often, and the relationship was stronger for more skilled players. The result suggests that expertise can produce rapid, effective recognition of familiar game patterns. Additional time did not automatically improve the decision. However, the finding is specific to the task and trained players; it does not establish that first impressions are always correct or that deliberation is unnecessary.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the task, the expert-versus-skill relationship, and why the study does not justify blind reliance on intuition."
Key point:
- Fast first choices can reflect learned pattern recognition.
- More deliberation is not always better in familiar expert tasks.
Source links:
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
23. Q: How should a performer decide whether to trust an initial response or slow down and analyze?
A: Show answer
The performer should assess familiarity, time available, stakes, and the quality of prior training. If the situation closely matches patterns repeatedly practiced, a rapid response may draw on reliable expertise. If the situation is new, ambiguous, or unusually consequential, deliberate checking may identify risks that automatic habits miss. A performer should also consider whether fatigue, bias, or incomplete information makes intuition unreliable. The chapter contrasts familiar handball decisions with novel choices such as an unusual golf situation. The practical rule is not 'always trust instinct' or 'always analyze'; it is to select the mode of control that fits the problem.
Study instruction:
- "Create a decision checklist using familiarity, novelty, available time, consequence, and evidence quality."
Key point:
- Expertise supports rapid decisions in familiar patterns.
- Novelty and uncertainty can justify deliberate analysis.
Source links:
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
24. Q: How does practice change the brain's support for a motor skill?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses research in which performance of a repeated sequence became faster and more automatic with training. Early in learning, a broad network that includes prefrontal and other attention-demanding regions contributes to step-by-step control. After extensive practice, performance can require less activity in some of these regions, reflecting greater efficiency and reduced dependence on conscious supervision. This does not mean the brain stops working; the pattern of recruitment changes as the skill becomes learned. The practical implication is that practice should build stable coordination so that execution does not require constant verbal monitoring.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how control demands differ between early learning and practiced execution."
Key point:
- Practice can reduce the need for effortful executive control.
- Automaticity reflects more efficient organization, not absence of brain activity.
Source links:
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
25. Q: What did the Poldrack motor-automaticity study contribute to the chapter's argument?
A: Show answer
The chapter cites Poldrack and colleagues' fMRI research on learning a repeating response sequence. Participants practiced the task and were tested under single-task and dual-task conditions; reduced interference from a secondary task was used as an indicator of automaticity. With training, performance became more automatic and activity in several frontal and striatal regions changed, consistent with reduced executive demands and stronger representation of learned sequences. The study supports the idea that practice changes the neural organization of performance. It does not show that every sport skill uses identical circuits or that one brain region alone causes choking.
Study instruction:
- "Review the study's task, its measure of automaticity, and the difference between a laboratory sequence task and a real sport."
Key point:
- Research links practice with changing neural demands.
- Laboratory evidence supports a mechanism but must be applied cautiously to real performance.
Source links:
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
26. Q: What are the limitations of using brain imaging to explain skilled performance?
A: Show answer
Brain imaging can show which regions change activity during a task, but it does not by itself prove that a particular region causes a performance outcome. A simple button-press sequence in a scanner is not equivalent to a golf swing, musical performance, or competitive match. Scanner constraints also limit the movements participants can perform. The chapter uses imaging to illustrate changing control demands, not to claim that all skilled performance has a single neural signature. Good interpretation distinguishes correlation, task design, sample, and ecological validity. Coaches should combine laboratory findings with observation of actual performance.
Study instruction:
- "For a brain-imaging claim, identify the task, sample, measured signal, and limits on generalizing to real-world performance."
Key point:
- Neural activation patterns are evidence, not complete causal explanations.
- Laboratory findings require careful translation into sport and performance settings.
Source links:
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
27. Q: What did EEG research suggest about expert and novice performers before a movement?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes work by Bradley Hatfield comparing brain activity in experienced marksmen and beginners. Under normal conditions, experts showed a quieter pattern of activity during the aiming period and a decrease in communication between motor areas and regions associated with monitoring and control just before the shot. Beginners showed a different pattern, with more widespread activity. The interpretation is that experts may execute a well-practiced action more efficiently when they do not over-involve conscious monitoring systems. EEG measures electrical activity at the scalp and provides a different kind of evidence from fMRI; it does not directly reveal every underlying neural process.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the chapter's EEG findings for experts and novices and explain what reduced pre-shot activity may indicate."
Key point:
- Experts can execute learned actions with less unnecessary neural involvement.
- EEG patterns are indirect measures and should not be overinterpreted.
Source links:
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
28. Q: Why can increased brain coherence under pressure be associated with poorer expert performance?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes studies in which skilled dart throwers and ROTC cadets showed increased coherence between motor regions and areas involved in attention, memory, and control when pressure was introduced. This pattern accompanied greater anxiety and less accurate performance. One interpretation is that pressure re-engages monitoring systems that are less involved during fluent execution, adding interference to a practiced action. Coherence is a measure of coordinated signal relationships, not a simple score of whether the brain is functioning well. More connectivity is not always better; the useful pattern depends on the task and stage of performance.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why increased coordination between brain regions may be unhelpful during a highly automated action."
Key point:
- More brain activity is not automatically better performance.
- The appropriate level of monitoring depends on task demands and expertise.
Source links:
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
29. Q: What is self-consciousness, and how can it influence performance under pressure?
A: Show answer
Self-consciousness refers to a tendency to monitor one's own thoughts, appearance, actions, or the way others may evaluate the person. The chapter reports that highly self-conscious performers may be more vulnerable to choking because pressure intensifies attention to their own performance. Instead of allowing a learned skill to operate, they may inspect each movement and attempt to correct it in real time. This tendency can affect sports, music, and other public performance settings. It is a risk factor, not a deterministic diagnosis: the effect depends on the task, context, skill level, and the person's ability to direct attention constructively.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the kinds of self-monitoring that increase under evaluation and determine whether they help or interfere with execution."
Key point:
- Self-consciousness can amplify pressure-related self-focus.
- Individual tendencies interact with the demands of the task.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
30. Q: What is reinvestment in the context of skilled performance?
A: Show answer
Reinvestment is the return of conscious attention to explicit rules or movement details that were important during early learning but are no longer needed for fluent execution. A golfer may consciously reconstruct the grip, backswing, wrist angle, and follow-through while trying to prevent a mistake. The chapter describes a Reinvestment Scale designed to assess tendencies such as repeated self-reflection and concern about how one is perceived. A higher tendency may indicate vulnerability to overthinking, but it should not be treated as a clinical diagnosis or a guaranteed prediction of failure. It is one way researchers study individual differences in pressure responses.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how explicit movement rules can re-enter an otherwise automated skill and why that may matter under pressure."
Key point:
- Reinvestment brings explicit control back into skilled execution.
- It may increase the risk of over-monitoring in pressure situations.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
31. Q: How should the Reinvestment Scale be interpreted without overclaiming?
A: Show answer
The scale uses self-report items about reflection, concern with presentation, awareness of one's own thinking, and repeated attention to past upsetting events. It can help researchers examine whether people who habitually monitor themselves are more likely to experience skill breakdown under pressure. However, self-report is affected by memory, interpretation, and context. A score does not prove that a person will choke, identify the cause of a particular error, or substitute for observation of actual performance. The chapter distinguishes a research measure from a practical certainty. Coaches can use the concept to prompt reflection, but should avoid labeling athletes or treating a questionnaire as a diagnosis.
Study instruction:
- "Review what a self-report scale measures and list at least two reasons why its score cannot predict every performance outcome."
Key point:
- A scale estimates a tendency, not an inevitable outcome.
- Combine self-report with task-specific observation and performance evidence.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
32. Q: Why might highly celebrated superstars be vulnerable in decisive moments?
A: Show answer
Public status can increase expectations, scrutiny, and the perceived cost of failure. The chapter describes analysis of international football penalty shootouts in which already celebrated players performed less successfully than players who later became prominent. The proposed explanation is that established superstars may feel greater pressure to protect their reputation and satisfy fans, increasing self-consciousness and attention to execution. This is not evidence that talent causes choking or that every star performs poorly. It illustrates how success can change the social meaning of a performance and therefore alter the pressure the athlete experiences.
Study instruction:
- "Separate underlying skill from the additional expectations attached to reputation and public status."
Key point:
- High status can increase evaluative pressure.
- Performance risk depends on how expectations affect attention and behavior.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
33. Q: What is stereotype threat, and how can it affect a skilled performer?
A: Show answer
