Stereotype threat is the risk that a person will underperform when a situation makes a negative stereotype about a group to which the person belongs salient. The chapter describes how a casual remark about women's strength affected a golfer's confidence and how task framing in laboratory putting studies changed performance. A person may worry about confirming a stereotype, become more self-conscious, and monitor movements more closely. The threat can operate even when the performer does not personally endorse the stereotype. It is a situational pressure mechanism, not evidence that the stereotype is true.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the stereotype made salient, the task affected, and the way the framing may alter attention."
Key point:
- Stereotype threat can change performance through situational evaluation.
- A stereotype's activation does not validate the stereotype.
Source links:
- Stone et al.: Stereotype Threat Effects on Black and White Athletic Performance
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
34. Q: What did Stone and colleagues' golf-putting experiment demonstrate about stereotype framing?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an experiment in which golfers were told that putting measured either sports intelligence or natural athletic ability. The framing affected Black and White participants differently in ways consistent with the negative stereotypes made salient in that study. The University of Arizona record summarizes two experiments showing that framing an athletic task as diagnostic of a negative racial stereotype could impair performance. The finding illustrates how the meaning assigned to a task can change the pressure experienced by a performer. It should be described as a result from a particular study, not generalized to every person or every sports setting.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the two task framings and explain why the study tested more than physical putting skill."
Key point:
- Task framing can activate identity-related performance pressure.
- Study findings are contextual and do not support broad claims about group ability.
Source links:
- Stone et al.: Stereotype Threat Effects on Black and White Athletic Performance
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
35. Q: How can stereotype threat lead to paralysis by analysis?
A: Show answer
When a performer fears confirming a negative expectation, attention may shift toward how each action will be judged. The person may monitor technique, rehearse possible mistakes, or try to control a movement that normally operates automatically. That response can create the same self-focus pathway associated with choking. The chapter notes that reducing time for excessive thought or introducing a task-appropriate distraction improved performance in the described putting research. These approaches should be evaluated carefully: distraction is not suitable for every skill, and it must not remove attention from safety-critical information. The broader aim is to reduce unhelpful evaluation and restore task-relevant attention.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the sequence from stereotype cue to worry, self-monitoring, and movement disruption."
Key point:
- Identity-related pressure can trigger self-focused control.
- Countermeasures should target the mechanism without compromising task awareness.
Source links:
- Stone et al.: Stereotype Threat Effects on Black and White Athletic Performance
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
36. Q: Why might playing faster or using a simple distraction reduce stereotype-related performance disruption?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that golfers sometimes performed better when they had less time to overthink or when attention was occupied by a simple secondary task such as counting backward. The proposed explanation is that these methods reduce the opportunity for excessive conscious monitoring of a practiced movement. They are not universal solutions. A distraction that competes with essential perception, safety, or decision-making could make performance worse. Coaches should test any method in practice, ensure that it suits the skill, and retain awareness of important external cues. The principle is to prevent unhelpful self-focus, not to distract performers indiscriminately.
Study instruction:
- "Design a safe practice test comparing normal execution with a brief, task-compatible attention strategy."
Key point:
- Reducing overthinking may help some automated skills.
- A distraction must not compete with information essential to the task.
Source links:
- Stone et al.: Stereotype Threat Effects on Black and White Athletic Performance
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
37. Q: What are the yips, and how are they manifested?
A: Show answer
The yips are involuntary or poorly controlled movements that interfere with a fine motor action, commonly discussed in golf putting. The chapter describes jerks, tremors, spasms, or freezing in the hands and forearms, and notes that similar problems occur in archery and precision occupations. The term is used broadly in sport, but the underlying causes are not necessarily identical in every person. Some cases are associated with task-specific neurological movement disorders; others appear more closely related to performance anxiety and excessive self-monitoring. The visible symptom therefore needs careful interpretation rather than an assumption that all yips have one cause.
Study instruction:
- "List the observable movement symptoms and explain why symptom description alone does not establish the underlying cause."
Key point:
- The yips describe a disruptive movement phenomenon.
- Similar symptoms can arise through different mechanisms.
Source links:
- Stinear et al.: The Yips in Golf—Multimodal Evidence for Two Subtypes
- Smith et al.: The Yips in Golf—A Continuum Between Focal Dystonia and Choking
38. Q: How do Type I and Type II yips differ?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents a two-subtype distinction. Type I is associated with focal dystonia or altered motor-pathway function and is characterized primarily by movement-related symptoms. Type II is more closely associated with performance anxiety, heightened self-awareness, and paralysis by analysis. Stinear and colleagues reported evidence differentiating golfers who mainly described movement-related symptoms from those whose symptoms were more anxiety-related. The distinction matters because a purely psychological explanation may overlook a neurological condition, while a purely neurological explanation may overlook the role of anxiety and self-focus. Some individuals may show overlapping features rather than fitting neatly into one category.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the proposed mechanisms, typical signs, and implications of Type I and Type II yips."
Key point:
- The yips are not one uniform condition.
- Assessment should consider both motor and psychological features.
Source links:
- Stinear et al.: The Yips in Golf—Multimodal Evidence for Two Subtypes
- Smith et al.: The Yips in Golf—A Continuum Between Focal Dystonia and Choking
39. Q: Why should the yips not automatically be treated as ordinary performance anxiety?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that some yips may involve focal dystonia, a neurological movement disorder associated with involuntary muscle activation and task-specific motor disturbance. A golfer who experiences a repeated involuntary jerk or tremor may not be able to correct the problem simply by becoming more confident or relaxing. Research cited in the chapter supports the possibility of distinct neurological and anxiety-related subtypes. Persistent, involuntary, or worsening symptoms should therefore be assessed by an appropriately qualified health professional. The educational distinction is important, but a study guide cannot diagnose an individual or recommend a medical treatment.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why persistent involuntary symptoms warrant assessment beyond ordinary performance coaching."
Key point:
- Not every yip is caused by anxiety.
- Clinical assessment is needed when a movement disorder is suspected.
Source links:
- Stinear et al.: The Yips in Golf—Multimodal Evidence for Two Subtypes
- Smith et al.: The Yips in Golf—A Continuum Between Focal Dystonia and Choking
40. Q: How can repetitive precision work relate to yips-like movement problems?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that yips-like symptoms can occur in musicians, stenographers, dentists, and surgeons as well as athletes. These activities involve repeated, highly practiced, precise movements, often using the same muscle groups for long periods. In some cases, task-specific dystonia may be relevant; in others, performance anxiety and excessive monitoring may contribute. The chapter does not establish that repetition alone causes a disorder. It highlights the importance of understanding workload, movement pattern, stress, and individual susceptibility. A sudden loss of fine motor control should be investigated rather than dismissed as lack of effort.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the repeated precision movements in an occupation and distinguish workload exposure from a proven cause of symptoms."
Key point:
- Yips-like problems can affect non-sport occupations.
- Repetition is a context to assess, not a sufficient diagnosis.
Source links:
- Smith et al.: The Yips in Golf—A Continuum Between Focal Dystonia and Choking
- Stinear et al.: The Yips in Golf—Multimodal Evidence for Two Subtypes
41. Q: How have golfers used grip or technique changes to respond to the yips?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes golfers who changed their putting grip or posture to alter the learned movement pattern. Bernard Langer's grip and Sam Snead's sidesaddle approach are presented as examples of changing the mechanics so the performer does not simply reproduce the old action associated with the symptom. A different grip may change the sensory and motor demands and create a new pattern to practice. The chapter also notes that such changes do not work for everyone. They are examples of adaptation, not guaranteed treatment, and should be evaluated for legality, comfort, consistency, and the underlying cause of the movement problem.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the original and modified grip and explain how a changed movement pattern might affect learned execution."
Key point:
- Changing technique may help some performers establish a different action pattern.
- A grip change is not a universal cure for yips.
Source links:
- Smith et al.: The Yips in Golf—A Continuum Between Focal Dystonia and Choking
- Stinear et al.: The Yips in Golf—Multimodal Evidence for Two Subtypes
42. Q: Why might a technique change temporarily reduce a symptom without resolving its underlying cause?
A: Show answer
A new grip or movement strategy can alter the cues, coordination, and timing that trigger a familiar motor pattern. That may help a performer avoid reproducing an established error or symptom in a particular context. However, the underlying neurological or psychological process may remain, and the new technique may introduce other performance costs. If the problem is task-specific dystonia, changing equipment or grip may not address every relevant mechanism. If anxiety-driven self-monitoring is central, the performer may eventually begin monitoring the new technique in the same unhelpful way. A temporary improvement should therefore be evaluated over time and across conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Track whether a technique change improves symptoms and performance consistently, rather than judging it from one successful attempt."
Key point:
- A workaround may change the task without curing the cause.
- Monitor long-term results and the possibility of symptom recurrence.
Source links:
- Smith et al.: The Yips in Golf—A Continuum Between Focal Dystonia and Choking
- Stinear et al.: The Yips in Golf—Multimodal Evidence for Two Subtypes
43. Q: How should coaches and performers respond when yips-like symptoms appear?
A: Show answer
The first step is to describe the symptoms accurately: when they occur, which movement is affected, whether there is an involuntary jerk or tremor, and whether anxiety changes the pattern. Coaches should avoid shaming the performer or assuming the problem reflects weak character. Persistent symptoms should be referred for appropriate clinical assessment, particularly when a neurological movement disorder is possible. Performance support can separately examine routines, self-focus, workload, and practice conditions. A useful response combines observation, appropriate referral, and careful experimentation rather than forcing the performer to repeat the same failed movement under greater pressure.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an observation record covering the movement, context, frequency, anxiety, fatigue, and any technique changes."
Key point:
- Yips-like symptoms deserve careful assessment rather than blame.
- Separate clinical evaluation from performance coaching.
Source links:
- Stinear et al.: The Yips in Golf—Multimodal Evidence for Two Subtypes
- Smith et al.: The Yips in Golf—A Continuum Between Focal Dystonia and Choking
44. Q: Why can free throws become especially difficult in the final minutes of a close game?
A: Show answer
A decisive free throw concentrates responsibility and makes the outcome highly visible. The chapter cites analysis of NBA game records in which players tended to shoot below their career average when their team trailed by a single point in the final minutes. The pressure is not simply the distance to the basket; it is the meaning of the shot, the expectation of teammates and fans, and the lack of a truly equivalent practice environment. A player may worry about the consequence or begin controlling the mechanics of a familiar shot. The chapter uses this example to show how an otherwise routine action can become psychologically different under high stakes.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the physical demands of a routine free throw with the added social and outcome demands of a game-deciding attempt."
Key point:
- A familiar action can change psychologically when the stakes rise.
- Pressure can alter attention even when the physical task is unchanged.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
45. Q: Why is practicing a skill under pressure useful even though practice cannot perfectly reproduce competition?
A: Show answer
Pressure practice gives performers experience executing a skill while being observed, evaluated, or exposed to meaningful consequences. It can reveal how attention, routines, and movement change when stakes rise, allowing coaches to test strategies before competition. The chapter notes that practice cannot perfectly reproduce the exact pressure of a decisive match, but it can approximate some elements. Useful design features may include realistic scoring, time limits, observation, or consequences that are ethical and proportionate. The aim is not to create constant stress; it is to help the performer become familiar with relevant demands and preserve reliable execution.
Study instruction:
- "Design a practice drill that includes realistic evaluation while maintaining safety, fairness, and a clear learning objective."
Key point:
- Pressure practice can improve familiarity with evaluative conditions.
- No simulation fully duplicates the stakes of real competition.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
46. Q: Why can high-stakes practice never perfectly replicate the pressure of a real competition?
A: Show answer
A practice environment cannot fully reproduce the unique consequences, audience, uncertainty, history, and social meaning of a real decisive moment. A missed training shot may have little lasting impact, whereas a missed shot in a final can determine a title or affect a career. Even when coaches add incentives and observers, performers know the event is not identical to competition. The chapter therefore treats pressure practice as useful preparation, not a guarantee against choking. It can help expose vulnerabilities and rehearse responses, but performers still need to adapt to the actual conditions of performance.
Study instruction:
- "List which elements of competition can be simulated and which remain difficult to reproduce in training."
Key point:
- Pressure simulation is useful but incomplete.
- Preparation reduces uncertainty without eliminating the possibility of choking.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
47. Q: How does choking during decision-making differ from choking during movement execution?
A: Show answer
Decision choking occurs when pressure disrupts the selection of an action before movement begins. Jean Van de Velde's decision to attempt a difficult shot rather than choose a safer option illustrates this kind of failure. Execution choking occurs when the performer selects an appropriate action but the movement itself breaks down, such as a missed putt or free throw. The distinction matters because a decision problem may require better situational judgment, risk assessment, or inhibition of an impulsive choice, while an execution problem may require protecting automatic coordination from over-control. The chapter emphasizes that these failures can have different causes and should not be treated with one generic remedy.
Study instruction:
- "Classify a performance failure as a decision error, an execution error, or a combination and explain the evidence."
Key point:
- Not all choking occurs during movement.
- The remedy should match the stage at which performance breaks down.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
48. Q: How can a performer identify the mechanism behind a personal choking episode?
A: Show answer
The performer should reconstruct the episode without assuming that anxiety alone explains it. Ask whether the task required working-memory-heavy reasoning or a practiced motor routine; whether attention shifted to feared outcomes, the audience, or body mechanics; whether the situation was familiar or novel; and whether movement became rigid or involuntary. Compare the event with normal performance and note the precise point at which the breakdown occurred. Repeated observations can reveal patterns across practice and competition. If involuntary movement symptoms persist, clinical assessment may be necessary. The purpose is to select a response based on evidence rather than apply a generic confidence technique.
Study instruction:
- "Complete a post-performance review recording task type, pressure cues, attention, decision point, movement quality, and comparison with baseline."
Key point:
- Diagnose the process before choosing a countermeasure.
- Different mechanisms require different responses.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Stinear et al.: The Yips in Golf—Multimodal Evidence for Two Subtypes
- Smith et al.: The Yips in Golf—A Continuum Between Focal Dystonia and Choking
49. Q: How should attentional strategy change between learning a skill and performing it under pressure?
A: Show answer
During early learning, explicit attention to body position, sequence, and feedback can help build a reliable movement pattern. During technique correction, temporary conscious control may be necessary even if performance initially worsens. During fluent execution of an established skill, however, repeatedly checking every component can interfere with automatic coordination. The chapter therefore supports a stage-sensitive approach: use detailed feedback when learning or rebuilding, then simplify attention during performance. This does not mean ignoring the environment or abandoning technique standards; it means selecting cues that support the current objective rather than forcing all movement details into working memory.
Study instruction:
- "Create a three-stage practice plan separating initial learning, technique adjustment, and fluent performance."
Key point:
- Attention should match the stage of skill development.
- The same cue can help learning and hinder execution.
Source links:
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
50. Q: How can the chapter's findings apply to musicians and performers on stage?
A: Show answer
Musicians and stage performers face many of the same pressures as athletes: evaluation by an audience, fear of mistakes, reputation, and a need to execute rehearsed actions fluently. A singer may become preoccupied with being watched, while a pianist may consciously monitor finger movements that normally flow automatically. The chapter notes that performance anxiety can lead to excessive self-focus and that yips-like movement disorders can also affect musicians. Preparation should therefore distinguish deliberate practice from performance execution, and persistent involuntary symptoms should not be dismissed as ordinary nerves. Stage performers can benefit from observing exactly where attention shifts when the audience becomes salient.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one rehearsed stage action and distinguish practice cues from performance cues that preserve fluency."
Key point:
- The mechanisms of choking extend beyond sport.
- Audience evaluation can affect both attention and movement.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Smith et al.: The Yips in Golf—A Continuum Between Focal Dystonia and Choking
- Stinear et al.: The Yips in Golf—Multimodal Evidence for Two Subtypes
51. Q: What can coaches do to avoid turning support into additional evaluative pressure?
A: Show answer
Coaches can clarify that observation is intended to support learning rather than judge personal worth, give feedback on controllable task cues, and avoid adding unnecessary commentary during an automated movement. They can ask performers what type of feedback is useful at different stages and observe whether an audience changes execution. Support should not be confused with constant instruction. During learning, detailed feedback may be appropriate; during competition, repeated reminders about body mechanics may trigger self-focus. Coaches should also avoid stereotype-laden remarks and protect performers from humiliating or discriminatory evaluation.
Study instruction:
- "Review coaching language and identify comments that increase outcome pressure or direct attention unnecessarily to body mechanics."
Key point:
- Support is most useful when it fits the performer and task.
- Feedback should protect rather than disrupt skilled execution.
Source links:
- Stone et al.: Stereotype Threat Effects on Black and White Athletic Performance
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
52. Q: What are the limits of the chapter's explanations of choking?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes worry, self-focus, automaticity, audience effects, stereotype threat, and the yips, but no single mechanism explains every performance failure. Later reviews continue to debate how distraction and self-focus contribute, whether different tasks respond differently, and how experimental findings generalize to real competition. Individual differences, skill level, learning history, external conditions, fatigue, and decision demands can all matter. A careful reader should treat the chapter's mechanisms as explanatory models supported by selected studies, not universal laws. The correct analysis begins with the actual task and evidence from the performer rather than assuming that every error is caused by overthinking.
Study instruction:
- "Identify at least three factors that could explain a performance decline other than conscious over-control."
Key point:
- Choking is a complex, context-dependent phenomenon.
- Theories should guide investigation, not replace it.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
53. Q: How can the chapter's central ideas be turned into a practical performance review plan?
A: Show answer
After a pressured performance, compare the result with the person's normal capability and identify whether the problem occurred during reasoning, decision selection, or movement execution. Record pressure cues such as audience, reputation, time, and consequences. Note whether attention shifted to failure imagery, body mechanics, or external information; whether the performer chose an unsuitable action; and whether movement became rigid or involuntary. Then select a response that matches the mechanism: strengthen task knowledge for cognitive gaps, practice decisions for unfamiliar scenarios, protect automatic execution from unnecessary monitoring, or seek clinical assessment for persistent yips-like symptoms. Review the effect of any change across multiple sessions.
Study instruction:
- "Use a structured review form and repeat the analysis across practice and competition rather than relying on one incident."
Key point:
- Accurate diagnosis begins with comparison and observation.
- Intervention should target the mechanism, not just the visible mistake.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Poldrack et al.: The Neural Correlates of Motor Skill Automaticity
54. Q: What is the most important overall lesson of Chapter 7 about choking under pressure?
A: Show answer
The chapter's central lesson is that skilled performance can be disrupted when pressure changes how a person attends to, decides about, or executes a familiar action. Worry can consume working memory during demanding cognitive tasks; in automated motor skills, worry may trigger excessive conscious control, rigidity, and loss of fluency. Audience evaluation and stereotypes can intensify self-consciousness, while the yips may involve either anxiety-related breakdown or neurological movement disturbance. Expertise and practice support automaticity and anticipation, but they do not make a performer immune to pressure. The most useful response is to identify the task and mechanism, preserve helpful attention, and avoid treating every failure as the same problem.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter by explaining the distinct roles of worry, automaticity, self-focus, audience pressure, stereotype threat, and the yips."
Key point:
- Choking is a mismatch between pressure and the way a skill is controlled.
- Understand the mechanism before selecting a response.
Source links:
- Mesagno and Beckmann: Choking Under Pressure—Theoretical Models and Interventions
- Gray: Attentional Theories of Choking Under Pressure Revisited
- Smith et al.: The Yips in Golf—A Continuum Between Focal Dystonia and Choking
- Stinear et al.: The Yips in Golf—Multimodal Evidence for Two Subtypes

Table 8
Anti-Choking Techniques for High-Pressure Performance
1. Q: What does it mean to choke under pressure, and what mechanism does Chapter Eight emphasize?
A: Show answer
Choking is a deterioration in performance when the stakes are high, particularly when a performer responds by consciously controlling a skill that has become well learned. Pressure can increase arousal and worry; the performer then monitors movements that normally run smoothly without detailed supervision. That extra attention can draw working memory and the prefrontal cortex into execution and interrupt an established routine. The result is paralysis by analysis: the person knows the skill but performs it less effectively because they are trying too hard to manage each detail. The chapter does not claim that all pressure is harmful; it focuses on unhelpful responses to pressure.
Study instruction:
- Explain the sequence from high stakes, to worry, to excessive conscious control, to disrupted execution using a skill you have mastered.
Key point:
- Choking can occur when pressure changes how a well-learned skill is controlled.
- More conscious monitoring is not always better performance.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
2. Q: What physical and mental reactions to high-pressure situations can contribute to choking?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes increased heart rate, adrenaline, and a mind that begins to race with worries. A performer may think about failure, the people watching, or the consequences of a mistake. Attention can shift away from task-relevant cues toward self-monitoring and imagined outcomes. A faster heartbeat alone is not presented as the cause of failure; rather, arousal and worry can encourage the performer to intervene consciously in a skill that normally operates with less deliberate control. Recognizing this pattern helps distinguish the presence of stress from the response that may follow it.
Study instruction:
- Record physical signs and thought patterns you notice under pressure, then identify which thoughts pull attention away from the task.
Key point:
- Arousal and worry can redirect attention toward performance monitoring.
- The way a performer responds to pressure matters as much as the pressure itself.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
3. Q: Why can conscious attention disrupt a skill that has been practised to a high level?
A: Show answer
With extensive practice, some skills become organized so that the performer no longer needs to think through each movement step by step. Bringing conscious awareness back to those movements can recruit working-memory processes and the prefrontal cortex in ways that interfere with fluent execution. The performer may start checking posture, timing, grip, or individual movement components while the routine is unfolding. This can interrupt coordination and create hesitation. Beginners still need conscious attention to learn and correct technique; the risk arises when an expert applies beginner-style technical supervision to a skill that is already highly practised.
Study instruction:
- List details you once had to monitor consciously but can now perform with little deliberate thought.
Key point:
- Well-learned execution can be disrupted by excessive step-by-step monitoring.
- The useful level of conscious control depends on the stage of learning.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
4. Q: How can early recognition as a promising athlete increase performance pressure?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts a 2009 NCAA recruiting-rule change that moved the point at which basketball players could be considered prospects from the beginning of high school to the beginning of junior high. Although intended to close a recruiting loophole, the change made seventh- and eighth-grade players more visibly identifiable as prospects. Families could invest in trainers and camps, while players could feel expected to excel. Recognition may motivate a young athlete, but it can also make mistakes feel more consequential before the athlete has developed strategies for handling scrutiny and pressure.
Study instruction:
- Map the sources of pressure around a young performer, including family expectations, rankings, coaches, recruitment attention, and personal ambition.
Key point:
- Recognition and opportunity can bring additional performance pressure.
- Physical development plans should include preparation for scrutiny and stress.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
5. Q: Why is physical skill training alone insufficient preparation for high-stakes performance?
A: Show answer
David had spent thousands of hours practising football and conditioning, yet struggled during a state championship penalty shootout. His ordinary match performance did not guarantee that he could reproduce his skill when the outcome mattered and attention was concentrated on him. The chapter argues that performers also need to practise the mental and attentional conditions that accompany competition. This does not replace physical training; it integrates physical preparation with experience executing the skill while being watched, evaluated, interrupted, or placed under a meaningful consequence.
Study instruction:
- Audit a training programme and identify whether it develops only the physical skill or also rehearses the emotional and attentional demands of competition.
Key point:
- Ordinary practice does not guarantee transfer to high-pressure settings.
- Mental and situational preparation should complement physical training.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
6. Q: What does it mean to practise under pressure rather than merely repeat a skill in comfortable conditions?
A: Show answer
Pressure practice deliberately brings features of the real performance environment into training. The chapter recommends reducing the gap between practice and competition by adding realistic stakes, evaluation, time demands, or distractions. A basketball player might take free throws after an unexpected interruption; a musician might rehearse while being observed; a judoka might train in a smaller area or after demanding drills. The objective is not to make every session overwhelming. It is to let the performer experience manageable stress while still learning to execute the required skill.
Study instruction:
- Design one drill that reproduces a specific competition demand and state which feature of the real event it simulates.
Key point:
- Training is more transferable when it resembles actual performance conditions.
- Pressure should be introduced deliberately and in a manageable way.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
7. Q: How does repeated exposure to manageable stress help performers become accustomed to pressure?
A: Show answer
The chapter compares pressure training with exposure therapy for fears such as heights. Repeated experience with a simulated elevator ride can help a person become less distressed by height-related cues. Similarly, practising a skill under controlled stress gives the performer repeated contact with conditions that once felt unusual or threatening. The purpose is familiarity and habituation, not elimination of every stress response. A performer can learn that an audience, evaluation, or consequence for failure does not require abandoning the trained routine.
Study instruction:
- Identify a pressure cue you find difficult and plan a gradual sequence of safe practice exposures, from less demanding to more competition-like.
Key point:
- Repeated controlled exposure can make pressure cues more familiar.
- The aim is better functioning under stress, not a guarantee of feeling no stress.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
8. Q: What does Coach Roger Reid's free-throw drill illustrate about practising with consequences?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Southern Utah coach Roger Reid interrupting a scrimmage unexpectedly and sending players to the free-throw line. A made shot allowed the player to catch a breath, while a miss required a sprint around the court. The drill added a modest consequence and removed the comfort of knowing when free throws would be practised. The chapter reports that the team moved from a ranking of 217th when Reid arrived to first place by 2009, shooting just above 80 per cent. This illustrates pressure-aware practice, but does not prove that one drill alone caused the improvement.
Study instruction:
- Adapt the drill to a familiar skill by adding a proportionate consequence, an unexpected practice moment, and a clear success measure.
Key point:
- Modest consequences can make routine practice more competition-like.
- A reported improvement does not isolate the effect of one training technique.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
9. Q: What lesson does the British judo training example provide about narrowing the gap between training and competition?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes performance psychologist Pete Lindsay working with British Judo development athletes. Training became more demanding when athletes used a smaller mat area or completed demanding combat drills before a practice match. These changes were intended to raise pressure and make competition conditions less unfamiliar. The chapter reports three medals at the Junior European Championships and the team's strongest Junior World Championships result in fourteen years, including two bronze medals and one silver. The example supports pressure-aware preparation, but it does not establish that the training changes were the sole cause of the results.
Study instruction:
- Compare a normal drill with a competition-specific version and explain how space, fatigue, uncertainty, or evaluation could be adjusted safely.
Key point:
- Training can reproduce relevant competitive constraints without copying every event.
- Competition results should not be attributed to one intervention without further evidence.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
10. Q: Why can practising while being watched improve performance under later evaluation?
A: Show answer
Scrutiny can create self-conscious attention. The chapter reports that golfers who practised while being videotaped and expecting coaches to review the recording later performed better in a subsequent pressure situation than golfers who practised in isolation. It describes a similar finding among musicians who performed before an audience that included evaluators: prior practice under a camera's gaze was associated with better later performance. These examples suggest that observation is itself a relevant practice condition when the real event involves judges, coaches, selectors, or an audience.
Study instruction:
- Add a safe observation component to practice, such as a recording or invited reviewer, and note how your attention changes.
Key point:
- Evaluation and observation are performance conditions that can be rehearsed.
- Practice scrutiny should resemble the real setting without becoming needlessly intimidating.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
11. Q: Why must pressure practice rehearse the right situations and not just create general stress?
A: Show answer
The chapter's discussion of fighter-pilot training shows that experience is most useful when it prepares a person for the type of problem that actually occurs. Pilots handled malfunctions that they had practised more effectively than those that were not normally rehearsed. Simply accumulating hours or experiencing stress did not fully compensate for missing practice of the relevant response. Training should identify likely failure modes, decisions, and procedures, while also preparing performers to apply general principles when an event differs from the exact scenario rehearsed.
Study instruction:
- List the most consequential errors or disruptions in your activity and check whether training includes specific rehearsal of responses.
Key point:
- Pressure practice should cover task-relevant contingencies, not stress for its own sake.
- General experience cannot fully replace rehearsal of important responses.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
12. Q: How does the US Airways Flight 1549 example show the value of practising general emergency procedures?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts Captain Chesley B. Sullenberger's Hudson River landing after geese entered the engines in January 2009. The exact event may not have been a routine simulator scenario, but the broader problem of engine failure was familiar from pilot training. Sullenberger could draw on established procedures and checklists rather than invent a response from scratch. The chapter uses the incident to show why training should include both specific scenarios and general procedures that transfer to related emergencies. It does not suggest every unusual event can be predicted; rehearsed principles can support action when precise circumstances are new.
Study instruction:
- Identify the general procedure in a high-consequence task that would remain useful if the triggering event differed from the scenario practised.
Key point:
- Rehearsed general procedures can support responses to unfamiliar events.
- Training should prepare for known scenarios and principled adaptation.

13. Q: What is the “just do it” approach to a well-learned performance?
A: Show answer
The “just do it” approach means avoiding unnecessary delay and overthinking immediately before executing a skill that is already well learned. The chapter describes performers who use a short, consistent routine rather than extra time inspecting every movement. Australian-rules footballer Lance Franklin continued moving and took a free kick quickly; golfer Aaron Baddeley used a brief pre-putt routine. The author's laboratory found that skilled golfers putted better when instructed to act as quickly as possible. This is not a recommendation to rush every task; it is intended for mastered skills that can be disrupted by a long pause.
Study instruction:
- Time your preparation routine for a familiar skill and remove unnecessary pauses while preserving essential safety and setup steps.
Key point:
- A concise routine can protect fluent execution from excessive self-monitoring.
- Speed is appropriate only when the underlying skill is well learned.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
14. Q: Why can a delay immediately before a skilled action increase the risk of choking?
A: Show answer
A delay can create more time for worries and technical thoughts to enter awareness. The chapter discusses gymnast Alicia Sacramone, who was twice stopped before beginning her balance-beam routine at the 2008 Beijing Olympics; she then fell from the beam and later made errors in the floor exercise. It also describes skier Julia Mancuso's interrupted giant-slalom run at the 2010 Winter Olympics, after which she reported feeling emotionally exhausted. These examples illustrate how interruption and restarting can disturb readiness. They do not prove delay alone caused the errors, but show why timing and continuity may matter for highly practised skills.
Study instruction:
- Identify where your routine is vulnerable to interruption and practise a brief reset that restores readiness without adding technical analysis.
Key point:
- Unplanned pauses can invite worry and disrupt a familiar sequence.
- A simple reset is preferable to prolonged self-analysis.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
15. Q: What does “icing the kicker” mean, and why might it work under pressure?
A: Show answer
Icing the kicker is a tactic in which the defending team calls a time-out immediately before a field-goal attempt, forcing the kicker to wait or repeat preparation. The chapter argues that this can give the performer extra time to worry and consciously scrutinize a movement normally executed through a practised routine. It cites research by Scott Berry and Craig Wood on high-pressure NFL kicks in the 2002 and 2003 seasons, reporting lower success when a defensive time-out preceded the kick. The proposed explanation is that pressure and extra time can increase counterproductive attention. This is a context-specific finding, not a universal rule about every pause or kicker.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a time-out can change attentional state and distinguish the proposed mechanism from other possible reasons a kick may fail.
Key point:
- An interruption can create time for unhelpful worry and technical monitoring.
- The chapter presents icing as context-dependent, not guaranteed.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
16. Q: What evidence about time-outs before high-pressure NFL kicks does the chapter report?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that researchers examined 2,003 field-goal attempts across the 2002 and 2003 seasons; 1,565 were successful. Among 139 pressure kicks, 101 succeeded, about 73 per cent. When the defense called a time-out before a pressure kick, 24 of 38 attempts succeeded, approximately 63 per cent. The chapter interprets the lower success rate as consistent with pressure being compounded by extra time to dwell on the kick. These figures describe the seasons and sample discussed in the chapter; they are not current NFL statistics and should not be presented as a universal effect size.
Study instruction:
- Reproduce the reported percentages from the counts and explain why sample and time period matter when interpreting the result.
Key point:
- The chapter reports lower success after time-outs in the examined sample.
- Historical study results are not current league-wide rates.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
17. Q: What was “Icing 2.0,” and what uncertainty did the chapter identify about it?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes “Icing 2.0” as a tactic in which a coach calls a time-out from the sideline just before the snap, after the kicker has begun the apparent attempt. The kick is then declared not to count and must be repeated. Denver coach Mike Shanahan used the tactic against Sebastian Janikowski in 2007; the first kick appeared successful, but the re-kick hit a post and missed. The chapter says it was too early to determine whether the tactic worked and notes that the NFL might change its rules. The example illustrates how altered timing can disturb a skilled action, while distinguishing an anecdote from systematic evidence.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the original time-out tactic from Icing 2.0 and identify what evidence would be needed to evaluate the newer tactic.
Key point:
- Icing 2.0 attempts to disrupt a second execution after an apparent first attempt.
- The chapter treats its effectiveness as unresolved.

18. Q: Why is telling someone “do not think about it” ineffective for stopping overthinking?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes research showing that attempts to suppress a thought can make it more persistent. It uses the white-bear example: when people are told not to think about a white bear, the thought can become more prominent. Telling an athlete not to analyze a movement therefore provides no reliable alternative focus. The chapter recommends active attentional strategies that give the mind a specific task, such as attending to a visible feature of the ball, the target, or a rhythmic cue. These strategies redirect attention toward useful information instead of leaving the performer struggling to suppress unwanted thoughts.
Study instruction:
- Replace a negative instruction such as “do not think about your hands” with a concrete cue that directs attention toward a relevant object or outcome.
Key point:
- Thought suppression can make the unwanted thought more salient.
- A specific positive cue gives attention somewhere useful to go.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
19. Q: What are focus cues, and how can they keep attention away from disruptive technical monitoring?
A: Show answer
Focus cues are selected words, objects, or sensory details that direct attention toward something useful while a skilled action is performed. The chapter gives examples such as attending to golf-ball dimples, rugby-ball seams, or the point on a ball that a kicker intends to strike. A rifle shooter can attend to the target rather than the trigger finger; a singer can attend to the melody rather than the mechanics of a familiar high note; a microsurgeon can attend to the artery rather than each finger movement. A cue should be relevant and simple enough to use under pressure. Its purpose is to occupy attention without interrupting the learned movement pattern.
Study instruction:
- Select one external cue for a familiar skill and test whether it keeps attention on the task rather than on individual body movements.
Key point:
- A useful cue directs attention toward a relevant feature outside execution mechanics.
- Cues should be simple, task-specific, and practised before competition.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
20. Q: How does Tim Gallwey's “bounce” and “hit” cue illustrate rhythm-based attentional focus?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes tennis coach Tim Gallwey encouraging players to say “bounce” when the ball lands and “hit” when it contacts the racquet. The words mark important events and provide a simple attentional rhythm. Rather than instructing the player to control the racquet angle, elbow, or wrist, the cue keeps attention connected to the ball and the timing of the rally. This is a way to prevent excessive self-monitoring. It is not a universal formula: its value depends on whether it helps the individual stay engaged with relevant action and does not become another technical distraction.
Study instruction:
- Practise a two-part rhythm cue for a repeated movement and assess whether it supports timing without making the action feel forced.
Key point:
- Rhythm cues can anchor attention to meaningful task events.
- A cue should simplify attention rather than add another layer of control.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
21. Q: How did a golfer use a word cue to address the yips, and what does the example show?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a near-scratch golfer who struggled to initiate the downswing and repeatedly froze at the top of the swing. An intervention used the three-syllable word “Edelweiss” to match movement phases: “Ed” for starting the backswing, “el” for its top, and “weiss” for contact. The cue gave the golfer a rhythm that replaced repeated false starts. The example shows how a simple verbal structure can provide an attentional anchor when a movement becomes hesitant. It does not establish that the same word or syllable pattern would work for every golfer or every performance difficulty.
Study instruction:
- Create a short rhythm word or phrase for a repeated skill, align each part with a task event, and test it in low-pressure practice.
Key point:
- A rhythm cue can help organize attention when execution becomes hesitant.
- The cue must fit the individual's movement and be tested.

22. Q: What did the chapter's distraction studies suggest about skilled performance under pressure?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that skilled golfers in the author's laboratory made more putts in do-or-die situations when their attention was distracted from their movements than when they were left alone. In one study, golfers repeated words heard from a loudspeaker while putting. The chapter also describes Australian basketball players who made more free throws under pressure when listening to music. The proposed explanation is that distraction reduced detailed self-monitoring and allowed practised movement patterns to operate with less interference from working memory and the prefrontal cortex. These findings concern particular experiments; they do not show that any distraction improves every task.
Study instruction:
- Compare a familiar skill performed with ordinary self-monitoring against a safe external-attention task, recording accuracy and any loss of task awareness.
Key point:
- Some distractions can reduce overattention to well-learned movements.
- Distraction helps only when it does not remove essential task information.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
23. Q: What is the difference between a strategy focus and a technique focus?
A: Show answer
A strategy focus directs attention toward what the performer intends to accomplish; a technique focus directs attention toward how individual body movements should be executed. In football dribbling, “keep the ball close to the cones” is a strategic goal, while “keep your knees bent” is a technical instruction. The chapter argues that skilled performers may do better under pressure when they focus on the task objective rather than consciously supervising movement details they have practised extensively. Technique remains important during learning and correction, but during execution of a mastered skill, a strategy cue may be less disruptive than detailed internal instructions.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite three technical instructions as outcome-oriented strategy cues and identify when each type is most appropriate.
Key point:
- Strategy focus emphasizes the intended result; technique focus emphasizes mechanics.
- Skilled execution may benefit from attention to the task goal.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
24. Q: What did Robin Jackson's football dribbling study show about goal setting under pressure?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes skilled football players dribbling through cones after setting a goal. Some selected technique goals, such as keeping knees bent; others selected strategy goals, such as keeping the ball close to the cones. The players who focused on strategy performed better than those who focused on technique, including under pressure. The chapter uses this result to illustrate that skilled performers may undermine performance by concentrating on movement details they already know how to execute. The result supports a strategy-focused approach for the studied skilled players, not a rule that technical instruction is always harmful.
Study instruction:
- Design a movement drill with one technique goal and one strategy goal, then compare how each affects fluent execution under mild pressure.
Key point:
- Strategy goals outperformed technique goals for the skilled dribblers in the described study.
- Goal wording can influence where attention is directed during execution.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
25. Q: What is the “paradox of control” described in Chapter Eight?
A: Show answer
The paradox of control is that an attempt to improve performance by consciously controlling more details can make a skilled performer perform worse. The performer assumes that close attention to posture, timing, or body position will prevent mistakes, but those details may already be handled by a well-practised system. Bringing them into working memory can interrupt the routine and create hesitation. The chapter's examples include football dribbling goals and golfers who perform better with holistic rather than body-part-focused words. The point is not that monitoring is always useless; the appropriate level of control depends on learning stage and task demands.
Study instruction:
- Identify a moment when adding instructions made a familiar task less fluent and distinguish necessary correction from counterproductive micromanagement.
Key point:
- More control can reduce performance when it interferes with a mastered routine.
- The best attentional approach depends on skill level and task demands.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
26. Q: Why can a one-word outcome cue such as “smooth” or “net” help a skilled performer?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes research in which skilled golfers putted while thinking of a holistic word such as “smooth” rather than technical words such as “head,” “knees,” or “arms.” The holistic focus was associated with better putting in practice, and when pressure was increased by offering money to the best putters, their performance was not harmed in the same way as that of technique-focused performers. An outcome word can summarize the intended action and direct attention toward the result. The chapter suggests that seeing the target can cue the motor processes needed to produce it. A short word is useful only when it captures the goal and reduces unnecessary step-by-step monitoring.
Study instruction:
- Choose one concise outcome word for a mastered skill, practise it consistently, and check whether it supports a smooth routine without becoming distracting.
Key point:
- A holistic cue can keep attention on the result rather than separate body parts.
- The word should express the task outcome and fit the performer's routine.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
27. Q: What is an external focus of attention, and how does it differ from an internal focus?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes external focus as attention directed toward movement effects or the environment, such as the empty net or the next ski gate. Internal focus directs attention toward the body and its mechanics. Drawing on sports scientist Gabriele Wulf, the chapter suggests that focusing on where the performer wants to go can help the trained brain and body organize the action. This is especially relevant for well-learned skills. External focus does not mean ignoring the body during learning or correction; it means choosing the attentional target that best supports execution at the moment.
Study instruction:
- Convert an internal body-part cue into an external cue about the movement's effect on an object, target, or environment.
Key point:
- External focus attends to the intended effect; internal focus attends to body mechanics.
- For mastered skills, an external goal can reduce self-monitoring.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
28. Q: Why can asking an expert to describe a well-learned action interfere with later performance?
A: Show answer
Highly skilled actions may be performed largely outside conscious awareness. Asking an expert to describe detailed steps can force attention back onto movements that normally run automatically. The chapter reports a study by Mike Anderson and Kristin Flegal in which skilled and beginning golfers putted, then either described their recent putts or completed an unrelated task. Skilled golfers who described their putts later needed twice as many attempts to sink them as skilled golfers who had not verbalized their actions. Beginning golfers were not disrupted and improved slightly. The findings suggest that verbal analysis can affect experts and beginners differently.
Study instruction:
- Compare how a beginner and an experienced performer explain the same skill and note whether detailed analysis helps learning or disrupts fluent execution.
Key point:
- Verbalizing a mastered action can disrupt an expert's later performance.
- The effect may differ for beginners who still need conscious instruction.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
29. Q: Why does the chapter suggest that the best performers are not always the best teachers?
A: Show answer
An expert may know how to perform a skill without being able to explain the steps that produce it. Because much of the action operates outside conscious awareness, the expert may struggle to describe it in a way a beginner can understand. The chapter cites hockey player Thérèse Brisson, who preferred an experienced physical-education teacher over a highly skilled player for teaching youth skating. The teacher's coaching experience helped communicate how to skate faster, whereas playing experience alone did not guarantee the ability to explain it. Effective teaching requires diagnosis, sequencing, feedback, and language suited to the learner.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate an instructor by asking whether they can break a task into teachable stages, diagnose errors, and adapt explanations to a beginner.
Key point:
- Expertise in performing and expertise in teaching are distinct capabilities.
- Coaching includes explaining, observing, and adapting instruction.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
30. Q: How should a performer interpret a past choking episode without allowing it to define future performance?
A: Show answer
A poor performance can remain vivid and affect later events. The chapter describes a runner whose stumble at an important race could continue to haunt her, and Olympic shooter Lanny Bassham, who said he choked in 1972 but later studied champions and won shooting gold in 1976. The chapter warns against focusing only on what might be lost or repeatedly criticizing oneself. Failure should be examined for useful information, but it should not become a permanent prediction about ability. The practical response is to identify a specific correctable factor and direct attention toward the next performance.
Study instruction:
- Write a post-performance review that separates the specific error, its likely causes, the training lesson, and the next action from global judgments about ability.
Key point:
- A poor performance need not determine future performance.
- Learning requires analysis without prolonged negative rumination.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
31. Q: What three steps did the Canadian swimmers use to reframe a failed performance?
A: Show answer
The intervention developed by team psychologist Hap Davis and neuroscientists asked swimmers first to express the feelings they experienced while watching a failed race. They then identified what had gone wrong in the swim, such as being slow or needing to improve stroke length. Finally, they imagined changes for the next race. This moved the review from emotional reaction to specific diagnosis and future planning. The chapter reports that after the intervention, swimmers showed less emotion-related brain activity and more activity in motor regions when viewing the failure again. This supports structured reframing, not a claim that a short exercise guarantees better results for everyone.
Study instruction:
- After a disappointing performance, name the feeling, identify a specific correctable factor, and visualize one concrete adjustment for the next attempt.
Key point:
- Reframing combines emotional acknowledgment, diagnosis, and future planning.
- A useful review converts failure into an actionable next step.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
32. Q: What does the chapter mean by learned helplessness, and how is it relevant to performance failure?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses learned helplessness for a state in which people feel they have little control over an outcome and stop trying to reach the goal. In the swimmer study, researchers observed reduced activity in motor regions when athletes watched their failed performances before the intervention. The chapter compares this with responses in animals that appear unable to escape a situation. After swimmers expressed feelings, examined what went wrong, and imagined future changes, the reported pattern shifted toward less emotion-related activity and more activity in motor regions. The practical implication is to restore a sense of agency through specific, controllable adjustments rather than treating a failure as proof that future effort is pointless.
Study instruction:
- Identify one controllable factor after a setback and write a specific action that shows how the next attempt can be different.
Key point:
- Perceived lack of control can undermine continued effort.
- Specific next steps can redirect attention toward action.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
33. Q: How can negative self-talk affect performance beyond the event in which failure occurred?
A: Show answer
Focusing on what might be lost or repeatedly criticizing oneself can affect more than the immediate performance. Negative thoughts may keep failure emotionally active, increase anxiety about the next attempt, and draw attention away from the task. The swimmer example illustrates how a poor race can influence later events when the athlete continues dwelling on it. The chapter describes reviewing a bad performance promptly, identifying what needs correction, and moving attention toward the next event. This is not pretending the failure did not happen; it is acknowledging it, learning from it, and preventing its emotional residue from dominating the next performance.
Study instruction:
- Track the language you use after an error and replace global self-criticism with a specific correction and a next-attempt cue.
Key point:
- Negative self-talk can carry a setback into later performances.
- Constructive review acknowledges the error and redirects attention toward improvement.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
34. Q: What role did brain imaging play in the swimmer intervention described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
Researchers used fMRI while elite swimmers watched videos of their own failed races and videos of other swimmers. Before the intervention, viewing their own failures was associated with more activity in emotion-related areas including the amygdala, increased prefrontal activity, and reduced activity in motor regions involved in movement planning and execution. After the intervention, swimmers showed less emotion-related activity and more activity in important motor regions when watching the failed race again. The chapter interprets this as a possible shift away from negative emotional processing and toward readiness for action. These observations do not establish that fMRI diagnoses choking or that the intervention works identically for everyone.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the before-and-after pattern and distinguish measured brain activity from the researchers' interpretation of its performance implications.
Key point:
- The study linked reframing with changes in observed brain-activity patterns.
- Brain-imaging findings must be interpreted within the limits of the research.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
35. Q: What are beta-blockers, and why does Chapter Eight discuss them in relation to choking?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes beta-blockers as drugs that lower blood pressure by blocking certain sympathetic nervous system receptors involved in adrenaline effects. In the examples discussed, they can reduce physical signs such as hand tremor and a trembling voice. Such effects may matter in precision activities including pistol shooting, archery, music performance, or public speaking. The chapter uses North Korean pistol shooter Kim Jong Su, who was disqualified at the 2008 Beijing Olympics after testing positive for propranolol, to illustrate that beta-blockers can be prohibited in particular sports. The discussion is descriptive, not medical advice or an endorsement of drug use for performance.
Study instruction:
- Explain the chapter's description of beta-blockers and why reduced tremor might matter in a precision task, without treating it as a medication recommendation.
Key point:
- Beta-blockers can affect physical signs associated with adrenaline and anxiety.
- Their use may be restricted and requires medical and regulatory consideration.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
36. Q: What did the Dutch marksmen study suggest about beta-blockers and precision shooting?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a double-blind study from the mid-1980s involving about thirty skilled marksmen. Participants shot a standard 25-millimetre pistol after receiving metoprolol or a placebo; neither researchers nor shooters knew who received which treatment. The chapter reports shooting performance was about 13 per cent better among marksmen given metoprolol. It also notes the improvement was not related to changes in heart rate or blood pressure; reduced hand tremor was proposed as the explanation. This is a historical example in a specific precision-sport context, not evidence that medication is appropriate for an individual or other activity.
Study instruction:
- Identify the study design, comparison group, reported result, and proposed mechanism, then state why it should not be generalized beyond the studied setting.
Key point:
- The chapter reports improved shooting in the metoprolol group in one small study.
- Reduced tremor was proposed as the mechanism, not a universal performance effect.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
37. Q: What did the chapter report about beta-blockers and stage fright among musicians?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes string players from several London colleges who received oxprenolol and performed while professional judges scored them. The musicians trembled less and received higher scores overall, but the reported benefit was concentrated among players normally most affected by stage fright. Players who did not usually experience stage fright did not benefit in the same way. This suggests that interventions may affect people differently depending on existing vulnerability and task demands. The chapter also notes an ethical debate: some view beta-blockers as reducing debilitating anxiety, while others object to performance-enhancing substances in sport or artistic performance.
Study instruction:
- Compare the reported effects for highly anxious and less anxious musicians and explain why averages may conceal different individual responses.
Key point:
- The reported benefit was greater for musicians prone to stage fright.
- Individual differences and the ethics of performance medication matter.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
38. Q: What regulatory and ethical cautions accompany the chapter's discussion of medication for performance anxiety?
A: Show answer
The chapter places beta-blockers within a wider discussion of doping and prohibited substances at the Olympics. It notes that a substance may be restricted because it can confer an advantage or create risks, even when it is not an anabolic steroid. Beta-blockers were prohibited in some precision sports, including archery and pistol shooting, while their use was accepted in some musical settings. This shows that rules depend on the activity and its governing framework. A reader should not infer that a drug is safe, permitted, or appropriate for personal use. Medication decisions require qualified medical advice, and athletes must check current rules rather than rely on a historical account.
Study instruction:
- Identify the current governing body's prohibited-substance rules and the appropriate medical review process before considering a medication question.
Key point:
- Permission and restrictions vary by activity and governing rules.
- Historical examples are not a substitute for current medical or anti-doping guidance.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
39. Q: What does “flow” or being “in the zone” mean in the chapter's explanation of expert performance?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes flow, a concept associated with Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi, as a state in which a person is fully immersed in the activity and feels carried along by it. Being “in the zone” similarly involves reduced self-consciousness. Performers are absorbed by the task rather than occupied by anxious monitoring of each movement. The chapter uses golfer Kimberly Kim, who struggled to explain a strong tournament round and said she simply hit the ball, to illustrate that fluent expert performance may be difficult to describe afterward. Flow is not total unawareness; it is deep engagement with the activity.
Study instruction:
- Recall a task in which you became absorbed and identify which attentional conditions helped you focus on the activity rather than yourself.
Key point:
- Flow involves deep task engagement and reduced self-consciousness.
- Fluent performance may be difficult to explain through step-by-step analysis.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
40. Q: Why does the chapter caution against telling skilled performers simply to “get their heads in the game”?
A: Show answer
The chapter challenges this advice because it can encourage performers to become more conscious of movements best left to a well-practised routine. After David allowed penalty shots through his hands, his coach shouted that he should get his head in the game. Yet for a skill executed successfully many times, the goal may be to become less self-conscious rather than more. The performer still needs to attend to the task and make decisions, but not necessarily monitor every body movement. A useful coaching instruction should identify a task-relevant cue or strategic goal instead of demanding greater conscious effort.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite “concentrate harder” as a specific external cue or strategy goal suited to a mastered skill.
Key point:
- More conscious attention can be counterproductive for an overlearned action.
- Coaching cues should guide attention toward the task, not intensify self-monitoring.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
41. Q: How could a goalkeeper such as David apply the chapter's anti-choking techniques to penalty kicks?
A: Show answer
David's situation combines a practised skill, public scrutiny, high stakes, and a delay controlled by the kicker. A useful plan would include practising penalty saves under manageable pressure, sometimes with observers present. He could rehearse a short readiness routine for the waiting period and use an external cue, such as the ball or the shooter's approach, rather than mentally rehearsing every movement of his hands and feet. Strategy focus would help him decide what information to watch without micromanaging execution. After a difficult save or goal conceded, he could identify one specific lesson and prepare for the next attempt instead of carrying the previous error forward.
Study instruction:
- Build a goalkeeper practice session with pressure exposure, a waiting routine, one external cue, and a brief post-attempt review.
Key point:
- Pressure preparation should reproduce the goalkeeper's timing and attentional demands.
- A simple cue and reset can stop one mistake dominating the next attempt.

- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
42. Q: How should coaches balance technical instruction, pressure practice, and attentional strategies across learning stages?
A: Show answer
The chapter distinguishes beginners from highly skilled performers. Beginners benefit from time to think through a skill, receive technical feedback, and correct basic errors. As the skill becomes well learned, detailed instructions during execution may become disruptive. Coaches can then emphasize external cues, outcome words, strategy goals, short routines, and realistic pressure practice. They should also teach performers to review mistakes without prolonged negative rumination. The right approach is not to abandon technique but to match instruction to the learning stage: teach and refine mechanics during learning, then help the performer trust the trained pattern under competition conditions.
Study instruction:
- Classify a learner as beginner, developing, or highly skilled and select an appropriate mix of technical feedback, pressure exposure, and external focus.
Key point:
- Beginners may need conscious technical guidance; experts may need less during execution.
- Coaching should adapt as a skill becomes more automatic.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
43. Q: How can a performer combine pressure training, short routines, and focus cues in a preparation plan?
A: Show answer
Start by identifying the conditions that tend to trigger choking: an audience, time limit, consequence, delay, or high-stakes evaluation. Practise the relevant skill under gradually more realistic versions of those conditions. A short routine can reduce unnecessary waiting, while an external cue or outcome word can keep attention on the intended result. Training should include realistic contingencies rather than generic stress. After practice, review whether the cue and routine helped, identify one correctable issue, and rehearse that adjustment. Adapt the plan to the task and skill level rather than copying another athlete's method mechanically.
Study instruction:
- Draft a four-week outline identifying one pressure condition, one contingency, one brief routine, one focus cue, and one review measure each week.
Key point:
- Anti-choking preparation combines realistic practice with an appropriate attentional routine.
- The plan should be tested and refined using observable performance evidence.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
44. Q: Why is practising the exact movement alone insufficient when the real performance includes interruptions or scrutiny?
A: Show answer
A performer may execute a movement reliably in a quiet setting but struggle when competition adds a delay, audience, judge, fatigue, or unexpected change. The chapter's examples of Sacramone, Mancuso, golfers, and musicians show that surrounding conditions can change attention even when the physical skill is unchanged. Training should therefore include relevant contextual features, not only movement repetition. The goal is familiarity with demands that could otherwise trigger overmonitoring or worry. Contextual practice should remain purposeful because reproducing every possible distraction is impossible; coaches should prioritize conditions likely to affect execution.
Study instruction:
- Compare the real performance environment with practice and list the three contextual differences most likely to change attention or timing.
Key point:
- Context can affect execution even when the physical task is unchanged.
- Practice should reproduce the most important competition conditions.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
45. Q: What are the limitations of the anti-choking techniques presented in Chapter Eight?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that no training method is foolproof and that even accomplished athletes can falter. Pressure practice can improve familiarity but cannot guarantee success. A quick routine may help with a mastered movement but be inappropriate when safety checks are required or the performer is still learning. Distraction can reduce overthinking in some skilled tasks but may be harmful if it removes attention from essential information. Medication examples raise separate medical and regulatory questions and are not self-help recommendations. Strategies should be selected according to the task, skill level, evidence, and consequences of error.
Study instruction:
- For each technique, write one situation in which it may help and one in which applying it without adaptation could be inappropriate.
Key point:
- Anti-choking strategies reduce particular risks but do not guarantee performance.
- Task requirements, learning stage, safety, and individual differences matter.
Source links:
- Simon & Schuster: Choke by Sian Beilock
46. Q: How can a performer tell whether extra preparation is useful or has become counterproductive overthinking?
A: Show answer
Useful preparation clarifies the task, checks essential conditions, and establishes a plan before execution. Counterproductive overthinking repeatedly inspects details of a movement already learned, adds unnecessary pauses, or increases worry without improving readiness. The chapter suggests examining whether extra time makes the performer focus on body mechanics rather than the target or outcome. Comparing performance with and without a short routine or cue can provide evidence.
